or contradicted by
\tau(\vec{x})
and
\Sigma
.
A type
\tau
is said to be
realized
in
a model
M
if there is a
\vec{x}_{0}
in
M
satisfying all formulas in

\tau
.
\tau
is
non-principal
(with respect to
a first-order theory
\Sigma
)
if
there is no formula
\gamma_{0}(\vec{x})
such that
\Sigma\wedge\gamma_{0}(\vec{x})

entails all of
\tau(\vec{x})
.
The version of the omitting types theorem that we will
use states that

if we have a countable set
\Gamma
of complete types that are all non-principal relative to a complete
theory
\Sigma
, there
is some model
M
of
\Sigma
in which none of the types in
\Gamma
are realized.

Each of these results follows from a standard
model construction technique
[
Hod93
]
.

We
can easily show that to prove the multi-sorted
result, it suffices to consider only those
\Sigma
which are
complete theories.

Proposition 11
.

Theorem
10

follows from its restriction to
\Sigma
a complete theory.

Recall that we are proving the direction from implicit interpretability to explicit interpretability.
Our first step will be to show that
each element in the output sort is definable from
the input sorts, if we allow ourselves to guess some parameters. For example, consider
the grouping transformation mentioned
in Example
3.3
. Each output is obtained from
grouping input relation
F
over some Ur-element
a
. So each member of the output is definable
from the input constant
F
and a “guessed” input element
a
.
We will show that this is true in general.

For the next results, we have a
blanket assumption that our underlying language is countable
, which
will be necessary in some applications of the compactness theorem.

Given a model
M
of
\Sigma
and
\vec{x}_{0}\in{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
within
M
, the

type of
\vec{x}_{0}
with parameters
from
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
is the set of all formulas satisfied by

\vec{x}_{0}
, using any sorts and relations but only constants from
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
.

A type
p
is
isolated over
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
if
there is a formula
\varphi(\vec{x},\vec{a})
with parameters
\vec{a}

from
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
such that
M\models\varphi(\vec{x},\vec{a})\rightarrow\gamma(\vec{x})

for each
\gamma\in p
.
The following is a step towards showing that elements in the output are well-behaved:

Lemma 12
.

Suppose
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
is implicitly interpretable over
{\mathsf{S}}_{0}
with respect to

\Sigma
.
Then in any countable model
M
of
\Sigma
the type of any
\vec{b}
over
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
with
parameters from
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
is isolated over
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
.

Proof 4.2
.

Fix a counterexample
\vec{b}
, and let
\Gamma
be the set of formulas in
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
with constants
from
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
satisfied by
\vec{b}
in
M
.
We claim that there is a model
M^{\prime}
with
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}(M^{\prime})
identical to
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}(M)

where there is no tuple satisfying
\Gamma
. This follows from the failure of
isolation and
the omitting types theorem.

Now we have a contradiction of implicit interpretability, since the identity
mapping on
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
can not extend to an isomorphism of relations over

{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
from
M
to
M^{\prime}
.

The next step is to argue that every element of
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
is definable by a formula using
parameters from

{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
.

Lemma 13
.

Assume implicit interpretability of
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
over
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
relative to
\Sigma
.
In any model
M
of
\Sigma
, for every element
e
of a sort
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}
in
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
,
there is a first-order formula
\psi_{e}(\vec{y},x)
with variables
\vec{y}

having sort in
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
and
x
a variable of sort
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}
,
along with a tuple
\vec{a}
in
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}(M)

such that
\psi_{e}(\vec{a},x)
is satisfied only by
e
in
M
.

In a single-sorted setting, this can be found in
[
Hod93
]
Theorem 12.5.8 where
it is referred to as “Gaifman’s coordinatisation theorem”, credited independently to unpublished work
of Haim Gaifman and Dale Myers.
The multi-sorted version is also a variant of Remark 1.2, part 4 in
[
Hru14
]
, which points to a proof
in the appendix of
[
CH99
]
;
the remark assumes that
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
is the set of all sorts. Another variation is Theorem 3.3.4 of

[
AMN08
]
.

Proof 4.3
.

Since a counterexample involves only formulas in a countable
language, by the Löwenheim-Skolem theorem mentioned
above, it is enough to consider the case where
M
is countable.
By Lemma
12
, the type of every
e
is isolated by
a formula
\varphi(\vec{x},\vec{a})
with parameters from
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}

and relations from
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
. We claim that
\varphi
defines
e
:
that is,
e
is the only satisfier. If not, then there is
e^{\prime}\neq e
that satisfies

\varphi
. Consider the relation
\vec{j}\equiv\vec{j}^{\prime}
holding
if
\vec{j}
and
\vec{j}^{\prime}
satisfy all the same formulas using
relations and variables from
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
and
parameters from
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
.
Isolation implies that
e\equiv e^{\prime}
. Further, isolation of types
shows that
\equiv
has the “back-and-forth property”
given
\vec{d}\equiv\vec{d}^{\prime}
, and
\vec{c}
we can obtain
\vec{c}^{\prime}

with
\vec{d}\vec{c}\equiv\vec{d}^{\prime}\vec{c}^{\prime}
.
To see this, fix
\vec{d}\equiv\vec{d}^{\prime}
and
consider
\vec{c}
. We have
\gamma(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{a})
isolating the type
of
\vec{d},\vec{c}
, and further
\vec{d}
satisfies
\exists\vec{y}~\gamma(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{a})

and thus so does
\vec{d}^{\prime}
with witness
\vec{c}^{\prime}
. But then using
\vec{d}\equiv\vec{d}^{\prime}

again we see that
\vec{d},\vec{c}\equiv\vec{d}^{\prime},\vec{c}^{\prime}
. Using countability of

M
and this property we can inductively create a mapping on
M
fixing
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
pointwise, preserving
all relations in
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
, and taking
e
to
e^{\prime}
. But this contradicts
implicit interpretability.

Lemma 14
.

The formula in Lemma
13
can be taken to depend only
on the sort
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}
.

Proof 4.4
.

Consider the type over the single variable
x
in
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}

consisting of the formulas
\neg\delta_{\varphi}(x)
where
\delta_{\varphi}(x)

is the following formula

\exists\vec{b}~[\varphi(\vec{b},x)\wedge\forall x^{\prime}~(\varphi(\vec{b},x^{\prime})\rightarrow x^{\prime}=x)]

where the tuple
\vec{b}
ranges over
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
.
By Lemma
13
, this type cannot be satisfied in a model of
\Sigma
.
Since it is unsatisfiable, by compactness, there are finitely many formulas
\varphi_{1}(\vec{b},x),\dots,\varphi_{n}(\vec{b},x)
such that
\forall x~~\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\varphi_{i}}(x)
is satisfied. Therefore, each
\varphi_{i}(\vec{b},x)
defines a partial function from tuples of
{\mathsf{S}}_{0}
to
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}

and every element of
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}
is covered by one of the
\varphi_{i}
.
Recall that we
assumed that
\Sigma
enforces that
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
has a sort
with at least two elements.
Thus we can combine the
\varphi_{i}(\vec{b},x)
into a single formula
\psi(\vec{b},\vec{c},x)

defining a surjective partial function from
{\mathsf{S}}_{0}
to
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}

where
\vec{c}
is an additional parameter in
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
selecting some
i\leq n
.

We now need to go from the “sub-definability” or “element-wise definability”
result above to an interpretation.
Consider the formulas
\psi_{\mathsf{S}}
produced
by Lemma
14
. For a relation
R
of arity
n
over

{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
, where the
i^{th}
argument has sort
{\mathsf{S}}_{i}
, consider the formula

\displaystyle\psi_{R}(\vec{y}_{1}\ldots\vec{y}_{n})=\exists x_{1}\ldots x_{n}~[R(x_{1}\ldots x_{n})\wedge\bigwedge_{i}\psi_{{\mathsf{S}}_{i}}(\vec{y}_{i},x_{i})]

where
\vec{x}_{i}
is a tuple of variables of sorts in
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
.

The formulas
\psi_{\mathsf{S}}
for each sort
{\mathsf{S}}
and the formulas
\psi_{R}
for each
relation
R
are as required by the definition of
explicitly interpretable, except that they may use quantified variables and relations
of
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
, while we only
want to use variables and relations from
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
. We take care of this in the following lemma,
which says that formulas over
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}

do not allow us to define any more subsets of
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
than we can
with formulas over
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
.

Lemma 15
.

Under the assumption of implicit interpretability,
for every
formula
\varphi(\vec{y})
over
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{1}
with
\vec{y}
variables
whose sorts are in
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
there is a formula
\varphi^{\circ}(\vec{y})
over

{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
– that is, containing only variables, constants, and relations
from
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
– such that for every model
M
of
\Sigma
,

M\models\forall\vec{y}~[\varphi(\vec{y})\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\circ}(\vec{y})]

Proof 4.5
.

We give an argument assuming the existence of a saturated model for the theory: that is, a model
M

in which for every set of formulas
\Gamma(\vec{x})
, with parameters from the model,
of cardinality strictly smaller than the model, if
\Gamma
is finitely satisfiable in
M
then it is realized in
M
.
Such models exist for any theory under the generalized continuum hypothesis GCH.
The additional set-theoretic hypothesis can be removed by using weaker notions of saturation – the modification is a standard
one in model theory, see
[
CK92
,
Hod93
]
.

Assume not, with
\varphi(\vec{y})
as a counterexample.
By completeness and our assumption, we know that there is a
saturated model
M
of
\Sigma
containing
\vec{c}
,
\vec{c}^{\prime}
that agree on
all formulas in
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
but that disagree on
\varphi
. Call
R
the
reduct of
M
to
{\mathsf{Sorts}}_{0}
. The partial map that sends
\vec{c}
to

\vec{c}^{\prime}
is a partial automorphism of
R
. Since
M
is saturated, so is
R
,
so in particular
R
is strongly homogeneous and the aforementioned partial
map can be extended to an automorphism of
R
that sends
\vec{c}
to
\vec{c}^{\prime}
.
But then by weak implicit interpretability, this should extend to an automorphism
of
M
that sends
\vec{c}
to
\vec{c}^{\prime}
. This implies that
M
satisfies

\varphi(\vec{c})\leftrightarrow\varphi(\vec{c}^{\prime})
, a contradiction.

Above we obtained the formulas
\psi_{R}
for each relation symbol
R

needed for an explicit interpretation.
We can obtain formulas defining the necessary equivalence relations

\psi_{\equiv}
and
\psi_{{\mathsf{Domain}}}
easily from these.

Putting Lemmas
13
,
14
,
and
15
together yields a proof
of Theorem
10
.

4.5.
Putting it all together to complete the proof of Theorem
3

We summarize our results on extracting
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expressions from classical proofs of
functionality.
We have shown in Subsection
4.3
how to convert the problem
to one with no extra variables other than input and output
and with only monadic schemas –
and thus no use of products or tupling. We also showed how to convert the resulting
formula into a theory in multi-sorted first-order logic. That is, we no longer
need to talk about
\Delta_{0}
formulas.

In Subsection
4.4
we showed that from a theory in multi-sorted
first-order logic we can obtain an interpretation. This first-order
interpretation in a multi-sorted logic can then be converted
back to a
\Delta_{0}
interpretation, since the background theory forces
each of the input sorts in the multi-sorted structure to
correspond to a level of nesting below one of the constants corresponding
to an input object.
Finally, the results of Subsection
4.2
allow us to convert this interpretation
to an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression.
With the exception of the result in multi-sorted logic, all of the constructions are effective.
Further, these effective conversions are all in
polynomial time except for the transformation from an
interpretation to an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression, which is exponential time in the worst case.
Outside of the multi-sorted result, which makes use of infinitary methods, the conversions
are each sound when equivalence over finite input structures is considered as well as the
default case when arbitrary inputs are considered. As explained in Subsection
4.3
,
when equivalence over finite inputs is considered, we cannot hope to get a synthesis result
of this kind.

5.
The effective result: efficiently generating
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expressions from proofs

We now turn to the question of
effective
conversion from implicit definitions to explicit
{\mathsf{NRC}}
transformations, leading up to our second main contribution.

5.1.
Moving effectively from implicit to explicit: statement of the main result

Recall that previously we have phased implicit definability as an entailment in the presence of extensionality axioms.
We also recall that we can rephrase it without explicitly referring to extensionality.

A
\Delta_{0}
formula
\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)
, implicitly defines
variable
o
in terms of variables
\vec{i}
if we have

\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\;\wedge\;\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a}^{\prime},o^{\prime})~\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}~o\equiv_{T}o^{\prime}

(3)

Here
\equiv_{T}
is equivalence-modulo-extensionality, as defined in Section
3
.
It is the same
as equality if we add extensionality axioms on the left of the entailment symbol.
Thus
this entailment is the same as entailment with the sanity axioms from the previous section, using
native equality rather than
\equiv_{T}
. And since these are
\Delta_{0}
formulas,
it is also the same
as entailment over “general models”, where we assume only correct typing and projection commuting with tupling
.

Proof systems for
\Delta_{0}
formulas

Recall that our goal is an effective version of Corollary
4
.
For this we need to formalize our proof system for
\Delta_{0}
formulas, which
will allow us to talk about proof witnesses for implicit definability.

A finite multiset of primitive membership expressions
t\in u
(i.e.
extended
\Delta_{0}
formulas)
will be called an
\in
-context
. These expressions arise naturally when breaking
down bounded quantifiers during a proof.

We introduce notation for instantiating a block of bounded quantifiers at a time in a

\Delta_{0}
formula.

A term is a
tuple-term
if it is built up from variables using pairing.

If we want to talk only about
effective generation
of
{\mathsf{NRC}}
witnesses from
proofs, we can use a basic proof system for
\Delta_{0}
formulas, whose
inference rules
are shown in Figure

2
.

\frac{}{\Theta;\;\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\varphi,\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={Ax}}\frac{}{\Theta;\;\Gamma,\bot\vdash\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$\bot${-L}}}\\
\par\frac{\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\neg\varphi,\Delta}{\Theta;\;\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$\neg$}{-L}}\frac{\Theta;\;\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\Delta}{\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\neg\varphi,\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$\neg$}{-R}}\\
\par\frac{\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\varphi_{1},\Delta\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\varphi_{2},\Delta}{\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\Delta,\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}}\quad\textsc{left={$\wedge${-R}}}\frac{\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\Delta}{\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2},\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$\vee$}{-R}}\\
\par\frac{\Theta,y\in b;\;\Gamma\vdash\varphi[y/x],\Delta}{\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\forall x\in b\;\varphi,\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$\forall${-R}}, right={$y$ fresh}}\frac{\Theta,t\in b;\;\Gamma\vdash\varphi[t/x],\exists x\in b\;\varphi,\Delta}{\Theta,t\in b;\;\Gamma\vdash\exists x\in b\;\varphi,\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$\exists${-R}}}\\
\frac{\Theta;\;\Gamma,t=_{\mathfrak{U}}t\vdash\Delta}{\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={Refl}}\frac{\Theta;\;\Gamma,t=_{\mathfrak{U}}u,\varphi[u/x],\varphi[t/x]\vdash\Delta}{\Theta;\;\Gamma,t=_{\mathfrak{U}}u,\varphi[t/x]\vdash\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={Repl}}\\
\frac{\Theta[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x];\;\Gamma[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x]\vdash\Delta[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x]}{\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$\times_{\eta}$}, right={$x_{1},x_{2}$ fresh}}\frac{\Theta[t_{i}/x];\;\Gamma[t_{i}/x]\vdash\Delta[t_{i}/x]}{\Theta[\pi_{i}(\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle)/x];\;\Gamma[\pi_{i}(\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle)/x]\vdash\Delta[\pi_{i}(\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle)/x]}\quad\textsc{left={$\times_{\beta}$}, right={$i{\in}\{1,2\}$}}

Figure 2.
Proof rules for a
\Delta_{0}
calculus, without restrictions for
efficient generation of witnesses. The left side of
;
specifies the

\in
-context. The negation rules
\neg
-L
and
\neg
-R

permit to exchange formulas between both sides of
\vdash
such that it
suffices to have the rules for the connectives only for one side, where we
choose the right side.

The node labels are a variation of the traditional rules for first-order logic, with a couple
of quirks related to the specifics of
\Delta_{0}
formulas.
Each node label has shape

\Theta;\Gamma\vdash\Delta
where

•

\Theta
is an

\in
-context.
Recall that these are multisets of membership atoms — the only formulas in our proof
system that are extended
\Delta_{0}
but not
\Delta_{0}
.
They will emerge during proofs involving
\Delta_{0}
formulas when
we start breaking down bounded-quantifier formulas.

•

\Gamma
and
\Delta
are finite multisets of
\Delta_{0}

formulas.
1
1

1

Much of our machinery also works if sequents are built
from finite sets instead of finite multisets. However, the specification of
certain proof transformations (e.g. Appendix
G
) is much simpler with
multisets.

For example,
Repl
in the figure is a “congruence rule”, capturing
that terms that are equal are interchangeable. Informally, it says that to prove
conclusion
\Delta
from a hypothesis that includes a formula
\varphi
including
variable
t
and an equality
t=_{\mathfrak{U}}u
, it suffices to
add to the hypotheses
a copy of
\varphi
with
u
replacing some occurrences of
t
.

A proof tree whose root is labelled by
\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\Delta
witnesses that,
for any given meaning of the free variables, if all the membership relations
in
\Theta
and all formulas in
\Gamma
are satisfied, then there is a formula in
\Delta

which is true.
We say that we have a proof of a single formula
\varphi
when we have a proof of
\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash\varphi
.

The proof system is easily seen to be sound:
if
\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\Delta
, then
\Theta;\;\Gamma\models\Delta
, where we remind the reader that

\models
considers all models, not just extensional ones.
It can be shown to be complete by a standard technique (a “Henkin construction”, see Appendix
F
).

To generate
{\mathsf{NRC}}
definitions
efficiently
from proof witnesses will require a more
restrictive proof system, in which we enforce some ordering on how proof rules can be applied,
depending on the shape of the hypotheses.
We refer to proofs in this system as
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized proofs
,
To this end we consider a multiset of formulas
existential-leading

(
{\mathsf{EL}}
) if it contains only atomic formulas (i.e. formulas
of the form
t=_{\mathfrak{U}}t^{\prime}
or
t\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}t^{\prime}
),
formulas with existential quantification as top-level connective and the
truth-value constant
\bot
.

Our restricted proof system –
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized proofs – is shown in Figure
3
.
A superficial difference from Figure
2
is that
the restricted system is “almost
1
-sided”:
\Delta_{0}
formulas only
occur on the right, with only
\in
-contexts on the left. In particular,
a top-level goal
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash\Delta
in the higher-level system
would be expressed as
\Theta\vdash\neg\Gamma,\Delta
in this system.
We will often abuse notation by referring to
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized proofs
of a
2
-sided sequent
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash\Delta
, considering them as
“macros” for the corresponding
1
-sided sequent.
For example, the hypothesis of
the
\neq
rule
could be written in
2
-sided notation as

\Theta,t=_{\mathfrak{U}}u\vdash\alpha[u/x],\alpha[t/x],\Delta^{{\mathsf{EL}}}

while the conclusion could be written as

\Theta,t=_{\mathfrak{U}}u\vdash\alpha[t/x],\Delta^{{\mathsf{EL}}}
. As with

Repl
in the prior system, this rule is about duplicating a hypothesis with
some occurrences of
t
replaced by
u
.

A major aspect of the restriction is baked into the shape of the
\exists
rule.
It enforces that existentials are instantiated only in a
context which is
{\mathsf{EL}}
, and that the term being substituted is of a simple shape (tupling of variables).

\frac{}{\Theta\vdash x=_{\mathfrak{U}}x,\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$=$}}\frac{}{\Theta\vdash\top,\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$\top$}}\\
\par\frac{\Theta\vdash t\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}u,\alpha[u/x],\alpha[t/x],\Delta^{{\mathsf{EL}}}}{\Theta\vdash t\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}u,\alpha[t/x],\Delta^{{\mathsf{EL}}}}\quad\textsc{left={$\neq$},right={$\alpha$ atomic}}\\
\par\frac{\Theta\vdash\varphi_{1},\Delta\Theta\vdash\varphi_{2},\Delta}{\Theta\vdash\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2},\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$\wedge$}}\frac{\Theta\vdash\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\Delta}{\Theta\vdash\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2},\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$\vee$}}\\
\par\frac{\Theta,y\in b\vdash\varphi[y/x],\Delta}{\Theta\vdash\forall x\in b.\;\varphi,\Delta}\quad\textsc{left={$\forall$},right={$y$ fresh}}\frac{\Theta,t\in b;\;\Gamma\vdash\varphi[t/x],\exists x\in b\;\varphi,\Delta^{{\mathsf{EL}}}}{\Theta,t\in b;\Gamma\vdash\exists x\in b\;\varphi,\Delta^{{\mathsf{EL}}}}\quad\textsc{left={$\exists$},right=\text{$t$ tuple-term}}\\
\par\frac{\Theta[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x]\vdash\Delta^{{\mathsf{EL}}}[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x]}{\Theta\vdash\Delta^{{\mathsf{EL}}}}\quad\textsc{left={$\times_{\eta}$},right={$x_{1},x_{2}$ fresh}}\frac{\Theta[t_{i}/x]\vdash\Delta^{{\mathsf{EL}}}[t_{i}/x]}{\Theta[\pi_{i}(\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle)/x]\vdash\Delta^{{\mathsf{EL}}}[\pi_{i}(\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle)/x]}\quad\textsc{left={$\times_{\beta}$},right={$i\in\{1,2\}$}}\par

Figure 3.
Our
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized calculus for efficient generation of witnesses. The left
side of
\vdash
specifies the
\in
-context. The right side a finite multiset of

\Delta_{0}
formulas. Recall that an atomic formula is an equality or inequality
for
\mathfrak{U}
. Formula multisets
\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}}
are existential-leading, that is, they contain only atomic formulas
and formulas with existential quantification as top-level connective.

Soundness is evident, since it is a special case of the proof system
above. Completeness is not as obvious, since we are restricting the proof rules.
But we can translate proofs in the more general system of Figure
2
into
a
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized proof, but with an exponential blow-up: see Appendix
G
for details.

Furthermore, since for
\Delta_{0}
formulas
equivalence over all structures is the same as equivalence over nested relations,
a
\Delta_{0}
formula
\varphi
is provable exactly when
\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}\varphi
.

{exa}

Let us look at how to formalize a variation of
Example
1
.
The specification
\Sigma(B,V)
includes two conjuncts

C_{1}(B,V)
and
C_{2}(B,V)
.

C_{1}(B,V)
states that every pair
\langle k,e\rangle
of
V
corresponds
to a
\langle k,S\rangle
in
B
with
e\in S
:

\forall v\in V~\exists b\in B.~\pi_{1}(v)=_{\mathfrak{U}}\pi_{1}(b)\wedge\pi_{2}(v)\mathrel{\hat{\in}}\pi_{2}(b)

C_{2}(B,V)
is:

\forall b\in B~\forall e\in\pi_{2}(b)~\exists v\in V.~\pi_{1}(v)=_{\mathfrak{U}}\pi_{1}(b)\wedge\pi_{2}(v)=_{\mathfrak{U}}e\\

Let us assume a stronger constraint,
\Sigma_{{\mathsf{lossless}}}(B)
,
saying that the first component is a key and second is non-empty:

\begin{array}[]{l@{~}l}&\forall b\in B~\forall b^{\prime}\in B.\pi_{1}(b)=_{\mathfrak{U}}\pi_{1}(b^{\prime})\rightarrow b\equiv b^{\prime}\\
\wedge&\forall b\in B~\exists e\in\pi_{2}(b).\top\end{array}

With
\Sigma_{{\mathsf{lossless}}}
we can show something stronger
than in Example
1
:

\Sigma\wedge\Sigma_{{\mathsf{lossless}}}
implicitly defines

B
in terms of
V
. That is, the view determines the identity query,
which is witnessed by a proof of

\Sigma(B,V)\wedge\Sigma_{{\mathsf{lossless}}}(B)\wedge\Sigma(B^{\prime},V)\wedge\Sigma_{{\mathsf{lossless}}}(B^{\prime})\rightarrow B\equiv B^{\prime}

Let’s prove this informally. Assuming the premise, it is sufficient
to prove
B\subseteq B^{\prime}
by symmetry. So fix
\langle k,S\rangle\in B
.
By the second conjunct of
\Sigma_{{\mathsf{lossless}}}(B)
, we know there is
e\in S
.
Thus by
C_{2}(B,V)
,
V
contains the pair
\langle k,e\rangle
.
Then, by
C_{1}(B^{\prime},V)
, there is a
S^{\prime}
such that
\langle k,S^{\prime}\rangle\in B^{\prime}
.
To conclude it suffices to show that
S\equiv S^{\prime}
. There are two similar directions, let us
detail the inclusion
S\subseteq S^{\prime}
; so fix
s\in S
.
By
C_{2}(B,V)
, we have
\langle k,s\rangle\in V
.
By
C_{1}(B^{\prime},V)

there exists
S^{\prime\prime}
such that
\langle k,S^{\prime\prime}\rangle\in B^{\prime}

with
s\mathrel{\hat{\in}}S^{\prime\prime}
. But since we also have

\langle k,S^{\prime}\rangle\in B^{\prime}
, the constraint

\Sigma_{{\mathsf{lossless}}}(B)
implies that
S^{\prime}\equiv S^{\prime\prime}
, so
s\in S^{\prime}
as desired.

Effective Beth result

A derivation of implicit definability in our proof system
will be referred to as a
witness
to the implicit definability
of
o
in terms of
\vec{i}
relative to
\varphi
.
Formally, this is a derivation witnessing the judgement:

\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\;\wedge\;\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a}^{\prime},o^{\prime})~\vdash o\equiv_{T}o^{\prime}

With these definitions, we now state formally our main result,
the effective version of Corollary
4
.

Theorem 16

(Effective implicit to explicit for nested data)
.

Given a
witness to the implicit definition of
o
in terms of

\vec{i}
relative to
\Delta_{0}

\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)
,
one can compute
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression
E
such that for any
\vec{i}
,
\vec{a}
and
o
,
if
\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)
then
E(\vec{i})=o
.
Furthermore, if the witness is
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized, this can be done in polynomial time.

Application to views and queries

We now state the consequence for effective rewriting queries over views mentioned in
the introduction.
Consider a query given by

{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression
E_{Q}
over inputs
\vec{B}
and

{\mathsf{NRC}}
expressions
E_{V_{1}}\ldots E_{V_{n}}
over
\vec{B}
.

E_{Q}
is
determined
by

E_{V_{1}}\ldots E_{V_{n}}
, if
every two nested relations (finite or infinite) interpreting
\vec{B}

that agree on the output of each
E_{V_{i}}

agree on the output of
E_{Q}
.
An
{\mathsf{NRC}}
rewriting of
E_{Q}
in terms of
E_{V_{1}}\ldots E_{V_{n}}
is
an expression
R(V_{1}\ldots V_{n})
such that for any nested relation

\vec{B}
, if we evaluate each
E_{V_{i}}
on
\vec{B}
to obtain
V_{i}
and
evaluate
R
on the resulting
V_{1}\ldots V_{n}
, we obtain
Q(\vec{B})
.

Given
E_{Q}
and
E_{V_{1}}\ldots E_{V_{n}}
, let

\Sigma_{\vec{V},Q}(\vec{V},\vec{B},Q,\ldots)
conjoin the input-output specifications, as defined in Section
3
,
for
E_{V_{1}}\ldots E_{V_{n}}
and
E_{Q}
. This formula has variables

\vec{B},V_{1}\ldots V_{n},Q
along with auxiliary variables for subqueries.
A proof witnessing determinacy of
E_{Q}
by

E_{V_{1}}\ldots E_{V_{n}}
, is a proof that
\Sigma_{V,Q}
implicitly
defines
Q
in terms of
\vec{V}
.

Corollary 17
.

From a witness that a set of
{\mathsf{NRC}}
views
\vec{V}
determines
an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
query
Q
, we can produce an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
rewriting
of
Q
in terms of
\vec{V}
. If the witness is
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized, this can be done
in
{\mathsf{PTIME}}
.

The notion of determinacy of a query over views
relative to a
\Delta_{0}
theory (e.g. the key constraint in Example
1
) is a straightforward generalization of the definitions above, and
Corollary
17
extends to this setting.

In the case where we are dealing with flat relations, the effective version
is well-known: see Toman and Weddell’s
[
TW11
]
, and the discussion
in
[
FKN13
,
BCLT16
]
.

We emphasize that the result involves equivalence up to extensionality,
which underlines the distinction from the classical Beth theorem. If
we wrote out implicit definability up to extensionality as an entailment involving
two copies of the signature, we would run into problems in applying the
standard proof of Beth’s theorem.

5.2.
Tools for the effective Beth theorem

5.2.1.
Interpolation

The first tool for our effective Beth theorem, Theorem
16
,
will
be an effective version of interpolation Proposition
5
.
Recall that interpolation results state
that if we have an entailment involving
two formulas, a “left” formula
\varphi_{L}
and a “right” formula
\varphi_{R}
, we can get an “explanation”
for the entailment that factors through an expression only involving
non-logical symbols (in our case, variables) that
are common to
\varphi_{L}
and
\varphi_{R}
.

Theorem 18
.

Let
\Theta
be an
\in
-context and
\Gamma,\Delta
finite multisets of
\Delta_{0}
formulas.
Then from any proof of
\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\Delta
,
we can compute in linear time an extended
\Delta_{0}
formula
\theta
with
FV(\theta)\subseteq FV(\Theta,\Gamma)\cap FV(\Delta)

such that
\Theta;\;\Gamma\vdash\theta
and
\emptyset;\;\theta\vdash\Delta
.

The
\theta
produced by the theorem is a
Craig interpolant
.
Craig’s interpolation theorem
[
Cra57a
]

states that when
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
with
\Gamma,\Delta
in first-order logic, such
a
\theta
exists in
first-order logic.
Our variant states one can find
\theta
in
\Delta_{0}
efficiently from
a proof of the entailment in either of our
\Delta_{0}
proof systems.
We have stated the result for the
2
-sided system. It holds also for the
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized system, where the partition of the formulas into left and right of the proof
symbol is arbitrary.
The argument
is induction on proof length, roughly following prior
interpolation algorithms
[
Smu68b
]
.
See Appendix
H
.

We compare with the model-theoretic statement
Proposition
5
. There, the interpolant is

\Delta_{0}
, while here in the effective version it is
extended

\Delta_{0}
. This is due to the linear time requirement, which leads to the
involvement of equalities
=_{T}
in the interpolation algorithm. The
construction would also work in plain
\Delta_{0}
if
\equiv_{T}
is used
instead. However,
\equiv_{T}
is a shorthand for a formula whose size is
exponential in the term depth of the type
T
.

5.2.2.
Some admissible rules

As we mentioned earlier, our
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized proof system is extremely low-level, and
so it is convenient to have higher-level proof rules as macros. We formalize
this below.

{defi}

A rule with premise
\Theta^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\prime}
and conclusion
\Theta\vdash\Delta

\dfrac{\Theta^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\prime}}{\Theta\vdash\Delta}

is
(polytime) admissible
in a given calculus if a proof of
the conclusion
\Theta\vdash\Delta
in that calculus can be computed from a
proof of the premise
\Theta^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\prime}
(in polynomial time).

Up to rewriting the sequent to be one-sided, all the rules in
Figure
2
are polytime admissible in the
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized calculus.
Our main theorem will rely on the polytime admissibility within
the
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized calculus of
additional rules that involve chains of existential quantifiers.
To state them, we need
to introduce a generalization of bounded quantification:
“quantifying over subobjects of a variable”.

Definition 5.1
.

For every type
T
, define a subset of the non-empty words over the three-letter
alphabet
\{1,2,\ni\}
of
subtype occurrences of
T
inductively as follows:

•

If
p
is a subtype occurrence of
T
or the empty word then the concatenation

\ni,p
is a subtype occurrence of
{\sf Set}(T)
.

•

If
i\in\{1,2\}
and
p
is a subtype occurrence of
T_{i}
,

i,p
is a subtype occurrence of
T_{1}\times T_{2}
.

Given subtype occurrence
p
and quantifier symbol
\mathbf{Q}\in\{\forall,\exists\}
,
define the notation
\mathbf{Q}\;x\in_{p}t.\varphi
by induction on
p
:

•

\mathbf{Q}\;x\in_{\ni}t.\varphi
is
\mathbf{Q}\;x\in t

•

\mathbf{Q}\;x\in_{\ni,p}t.\varphi
is
\mathbf{Q}\;y\in t.\mathbf{Q}\;x\in_{p}y.\varphi
with
y
a fresh variable

•

\mathbf{Q}\;x\in_{i,p}t.\varphi
is
\mathbf{Q}\;x\in_{p}\pi_{i}(t).\varphi

when
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Now we are ready to state the results we need on admissibility,
referring in each case to the
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized calculus. Some
further rules, that are easily seen to be admissible, are used in the appendices.
All proofs are found in Appendix
I
.
The first states that if we have proven that there exists a
subobject of
o^{\prime}
equivalent to object
r
, then we can prove
that for each element
z
of
r
there is a corresponding equivalent
subobject
z^{\prime}
within
o^{\prime}
. Furthermore, this can be done effectively, and the
output proof has at most the same size: that is, there is not even a polynomial blow-up involved.

Lemma 19
.

Assume
p
is a subtype occurrence for the type of the term
o^{\prime}
. The following is polytime admissible

\dfrac{\Theta\vdash\Delta,\;\exists{r^{\prime}\in_{p}o^{\prime}}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}({T^{\prime}})}r^{\prime}}{\Theta,z\in r\vdash\Delta,\;\exists{z^{\prime}\in_{\ni,p}o^{\prime}}.\;z\equiv_{{T^{\prime}}}z^{\prime}}

Furthermore, the size of the output proof is at most the size of the input proof.

The second result states that we can move between an equivalence of
r,r^{\prime}

and a universally-quantified biconditional between memberships in
r

and
r^{\prime}
. Because we are dealing with
\Delta_{0}
formulas, the universal quantification
has to be bounded by some additional variable
a
.

Lemma 20
.

The following is polytime admissible (where
p
is a subtype occurrence of the type of
o^{\prime}
)

\dfrac{\Theta\vdash\Delta,\exists r^{\prime}\in_{p}o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}({T^{\prime}})}r^{\prime}}{\Theta\vdash\Delta,\exists r^{\prime}\in_{p}o^{\prime}.\forall z\in a.\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r\leftrightarrow z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r^{\prime}}

5.2.3.
The
{\mathsf{NRC}}
Parameter Collection Theorem

Our last tool is a kind of interpolation result
connecting
\Delta_{0}
formulas and
{\mathsf{NRC}}
:

Theorem 21

(
{\mathsf{NRC}}
Parameter Collection)
.

Let
L
,
R
be sets of variables with
C=L\cap R
and

•

\varphi_{L}
and
\lambda(z)

\Delta_{0}
formulas over
L

•

\varphi_{R}
and
\rho(z,y)

\Delta_{0}
formulas over
R

•

r
a variable of
R
and
c
a variable of
C
.

Suppose that we have an EL-normalized proof of

\displaystyle\varphi_{L}\wedge\varphi_{R}~\rightarrow~\exists y\in_{p}r~\forall z\in c~(\lambda(z)\leftrightarrow\rho(z,y))

Then one may compute in polynomial time an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression
E
with
free variables in
C

such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{L}\wedge\varphi_{R}\rightarrow\{z\in c\mid\lambda(z)\}\in E

If
\lambda
was a “common formula” —
one using only variables in
C

— then the nested
relation

\{z\in c\mid\lambda(z)\}
would be definable over
C
in
{\mathsf{NRC}}
via
\Delta_{0}
-comprehension.
Unfortunately
\lambda
is a “left formula”, possibly with variables outside of
C
.
Our hypothesis is that it is equivalent to a
“parameterized right formula”: a formula with variables in
R
and parameters that lie below them.
Intuitively,
this can happen only if
\lambda
can be rewritten to a formula
\rho^{\prime}(z,x)

with variables of
C
and a distinguished
c_{0}\in C
such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{L}\wedge\varphi_{R}~\rightarrow~\exists x\in_{p}c_{0}~\forall z\in c~(\lambda(z)\leftrightarrow\rho^{\prime}(z,x))

And if this is true, we can use an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression
over
C
to define a set that will contain
the correct “parameter” value
x
defining
\lambda
.
From this we can define a set containing the nested
relation
\{z\in c\mid\lambda(z)\}
.
A formalization of this rough intuition — “when left formulas are equivalent to parameterized right formulas, they are equivalent to parameterized
common formulas” — is given in Section
K
, where a similar
statement that does not mention
{\mathsf{NRC}}
is proven for first-order logic
(sadly it does not seem strong enough to derive Theorem
21
).

We now give the proof of the theorem.

To get the desired conclusion, we need to prove a more general statement by induction
over proof trees. Besides making the obvious generalization to handle
two multisets of
formulas instead of the particular formulas
\varphi_{L}
and
\varphi_{R}
, as well as some corresponding left and right
\in
-contexts,
that may appear during the proof, we need to additionally generate a new formula

\theta
that only uses common variables, which can replace
\varphi_{R}
in the conclusion.
This is captured in the following lemma:

Lemma 22
.

Let
L
,
R
be sets of variables with
C=L\cap R
and

•

\Delta_{L},\lambda(z)
a multiset of
\Delta_{0}
formulas over
L

•

\Delta_{R},\rho(z,y)
a multiset of
\Delta_{0}
formulas over
R

•

\Theta_{L}
(respectively
\Theta_{R}
) an
\in
-context over
L

(respectively over
R
)

•

r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}
variables of
R
and
c
a variable of
C
.

•

we write
\mathcal{G}(r_{i})
for
\exists y\in_{p_{i}}r_{i}~\forall z\in c~(\lambda(z)\leftrightarrow\rho(z,y))

Suppose that we have a

{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized proof tree with conclusion

\displaystyle\Theta_{L},\Theta_{R}\vdash\Delta_{L},\Delta_{R},\mathcal{G}(r_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{G}(r_{k})

Then one may compute in polynomial time an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression
E

and an extended
\Delta_{0}
formula
\theta
using only variables from
C

such that

\displaystyle\Theta_{L}~~\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}~~\Delta_{L},\theta\vee\{z\in c\mid\lambda(z)\}\in E\quad\text{and}\quad\Theta_{R}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}\Delta_{R},\neg\theta

The theorem follows easily from this lemma, so we focus on proving the lemma,
by induction over the size of the proof of

\Theta_{L},\Theta_{R}\vdash\Delta_{L},\Delta_{R},\mathcal{G}(r_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{G}(r_{k})
,
making a case distinction according to which
rule is applied last.
The way
\theta
will be built will, perhaps unsurprisingly, be very reminiscent
of the way interpolants are normally constructed in standard proof systems

[
Fit96
,
Smu68b
]
.

For readability, we adopt the
following conventions:

•

We write
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
for the multiset of formulas
\mathcal{G}(r_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{G}(r_{k})

and
\Lambda
for the expression
\{z\in c\mid\lambda(z)\}
.

•

For formulas and
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expressions obtained by applying the
induction hypothesis, we use the names
\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E^{\mathsf{IH}}

(or
\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}},\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}},E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}
when the induction hypothesis
is applied several times). In each subcase, our goal will be to build suitable

\theta
and
E
.

•

We will color pairs of terms, formulas and multisets of formulas
according to whether they are part of either
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L};\Delta_{L}}

or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R};\Delta_{R}}
either at the start of the case analysis or when
we want to apply the induction hypothesis.
In particular, the last sequent of the proof
under consideration will be depicted as

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\mathcal{G}

•

Unless it is non-trivial, we leave checking that the free variables in our
proposed definition for
E
and
\Theta
are taken among variables of
C

to the reader.

In two of the cases below, we will make use of some syntactic sugar on top of bounded quantification.
We introduce

\exists x_{1}\ldots x_{n}|t\in b\,.\,\varphi\;\text{ and }\;\forall x_{1}\ldots x_{n}|t\in b\,.\,\varphi

as notation. This will be an extended
\Delta_{0}
formula, intuitively quantifying
over variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
that occur in tuple-term
t
, which is bounded by
set
b
. Here
\varphi
is an extended
\Delta_{0}
formula. The variables other than
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
occurring
in
t
remain free in the resulting formula.

To make this precise, let
\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}(t,v,u)
be the set of the terms
expressed with projection applied to
u
that refer to an occurrence of
variable
v
in tuple-term
t
, ordered according to the occurrences when
t
is printed.
Formally,
\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}(t,t,u)\mathrel{:=}\{u\}
;
\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}(t,v,u)\mathrel{:=}\{\}

if
t
is a variable other than
v
; and
\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}(\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle,v,u)\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}(t_{1},v,\pi_{1}(u))\cup\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}(t_{2},v,\pi_{2}(u))
. We write

\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}_{i}(t,v,u)
for the
i
-th member of
\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}(t,v,u)
. We can
then define
\exists x_{1}\ldots x_{n}|t\in b\,.\,\varphi
as

\begin{array}[]{ll}\exists y\in b\,.&\varphi[\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}_{1}(t,x_{1},y)/x_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}_{1}(t,x_{n},y)/x_{n}]\;\land\\
&\bigwedge_{z\text{ is a variable occurring in }t\text{ other than }x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}}z=\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}_{1}(t,z,y)\;\land\\
&\bigwedge_{u\text{ is a variable occurring in }t\text{ and }\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}_{j}(t,y,u)\text{ for }j>1\text{ in }\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}(t,y,u)}\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}_{1}(t,u,y)=\mathcal{R}\mathit{ef}_{j}(t,u,y)\end{array}

\forall x_{1}\ldots x_{n}|t\in b\,.\,\varphi
can be defined analogously.

Here equality
=_{T}
(written above without the type decoration) of

extended

\Delta_{0}
formulas comes into play, in contrast to

\equiv_{T}
, to meet the polynomial time requirements of
Theorems
18
and
21

as indicated on p.
5.2.1
.

As an example for the notation consider

\exists x_{1}x_{2}|\langle\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle,z\rangle\in b\,.\,x_{1}\equiv x_{2},

which stands for

\exists y\in b\,.\,\pi_{1}(\pi_{1}(y))\equiv\pi_{2}(\pi_{1}(y))\land z=\pi_{2}(y).

With these conventions in mind, let us proceed.

•

If the last rule applied is the
\top
rule, in both cases we are
going to take
E\mathrel{:=}\emptyset
, but pick
\theta
to be
\bot
or
\top

according to whether
\top
occurs in
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}
;
we leave checking the details to the reader.

•

If the last rule applied is the
\wedge
rule, we have two cases according
to the position of the principal formula
\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
. In both
cases,
E
will be obtained by unioning
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expressions obtained from the
induction hypothesis, and
\theta
will be either a disjunction or a conjunction.

–

If we have
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}={\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2},\Delta_{L}^{\prime}}
,
so that the proof has shape

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi_{1},\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi_{2},\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2},\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

by the induction hypothesis, we have
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expressions

E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}
,
E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}
and formulas
\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}},\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}
such that

\begin{array}[]{c !\qquad!{\text{and}}!\qquad c}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi_{1},\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\vee\Lambda\in E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\neg\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi_{2},\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\vee\Lambda\in E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\neg\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\end{array}

In that case, we take
E=E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\cup E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\vee\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}
.
Weakening the properties on the left column, we have

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi_{i},\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},\theta\vee\Lambda\in E

for both
i\in\{1,2\}
, so we have

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2},\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},\theta\vee\Lambda\in E

as desired. Since
\neg\theta=\neg\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge\neg\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}
, we get

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\neg\theta

by combining both properties from the right column.

–

The dual case where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2},\Delta_{R}^{\prime}}
is handled similarly, except that we set

\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}
.

•

Suppose the last rule applied is
\vee
with principal formula
\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
.
Depending on whether

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}={\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2},\Delta_{L}^{\prime}}

or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2},\Delta_{R}^{\prime}}
, the proof will
end with one of the following steps

\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2},\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2},\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

In both cases, we apply the inductive hypothesis according to the obvious
splitting of contexts and multisets of formulas, to get an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
definition
E^{\mathsf{IH}}

along with a formula
\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
that satisfy the desired semantic property.
We set
E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
.

•

Suppose the last rule applied is
\forall
with principal formula
\forall x\in b.\varphi
. As in the previous
case,
depending on whether

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}={\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\forall x\in b.\varphi,\Delta_{L}^{\prime}}

or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\forall x\in b.\varphi,\Delta_{R}^{\prime}}
, the proof will
end with one of the following steps (assuming
y
is fresh below)

\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L},y\in b},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi[y/x],\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\forall x\in b.\varphi,\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R},y\in b}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\varphi[y/x],\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\forall x\in b.\varphi,\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

In both cases, we again apply the inductive hypothesis according to the obvious
splitting of contexts and multisets of formulas to get an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
definition
E^{\mathsf{IH}}

and a formula
\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
that satisfy the desired semantic property.
We set
E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
.

•

Now we consider the case where the
last rule applied is
\exists
. Here we have two main subcases,
according to whether the formula that is instantiated in the premise, that
is, the principal formula, belongs
to
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
or not. In the first case, we have two further subcases according to whether the instantiated formula
still has a leading existential quantifier or not.

–

If the principal formula is of the shape
\mathcal{G}=\exists y\in r~\forall z\in c.~(\lambda(z)\leftrightarrow\rho(z,y))

(so in particular,
\mathcal{G}
has a single leading existential quantifier)
and is
a member of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
,
the proof necessarily has shape

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}},{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}x\in c}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\neg\rho(x,w)},{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\lambda(x)},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}

\vee

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}},{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}x\in c}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\rho(x,w)\rightarrow\lambda(x),\tilde{\mathcal{G}}

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}},{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}x\in c}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\neg\lambda(x)},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\rho(x,w)},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}

\vee

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}},x\in c\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\lambda(x)\rightarrow\rho(x,w),\tilde{\mathcal{G}}

\wedge

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}},x\in c\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\lambda(x)\leftrightarrow\rho(x,w),\tilde{\mathcal{G}}

\forall

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\forall z\in c.~(\lambda(z)\leftrightarrow\rho(z,w)),\tilde{\mathcal{G}}

\exists

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}

where
x
is a fresh variable
So in particular, we have two strict subproofs with respective conclusions

\begin{array}[]{llcl}&{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L},x\in c},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}&\vdash&{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\lambda(x),\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\neg\rho(x,w),\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}\\
\text{and}&{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L},x\in c},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}&\vdash&{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\neg\lambda(x),\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\rho(x,w),\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}\end{array}

Applying the inductive hypothesis, we obtain
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expressions
E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}
,
E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}

and formulas
\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}},\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}
which contain free variables in
C\cup\{x\}
such that all of the following hold

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L},x\in c}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}\phantom{\neg}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\lambda(x),\Delta_{L}},\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\vee\Lambda\in E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}

(4)

and

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L},x\in c}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\neg\lambda(x),\Delta_{L}},\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\vee\Lambda\in E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}

(5)

and

\displaystyle\phantom{x\in c,}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\neg\rho(x,w),\Delta_{R}},\neg\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}

(6)

and

\displaystyle\phantom{x\in c,}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}\phantom{\neg}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\rho(x,w),\Delta_{R}},\neg\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}

(7)

With this in hand, we set

\theta\mathrel{:=}\exists x\in c.~\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad E\mathrel{:=}\left\{\left\{x\in c\mid\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\right\}\right\}~\cup~\bigcup\left\{E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\cup E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\mid x\in c\right\}

Note in particular that the free variables of
E
and
\theta
are contained in
C
, since we bind
x
.
The bindings of
x
have radically different meaning across the two main components

E_{1}\mathrel{:=}\left\{\left\{x\in c\mid\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\right\}\right\}
and
E_{2}\mathrel{:=}\left\{E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\cup E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\mid x\in c\right\}

of
E=E_{1}\cup E_{2}
.
E_{1}
consists of a single definition corresponding to the restriction of
c
to
\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}
, and there
x

plays the role of an element being defined. On the other hand,
E_{2}
corresponds to the joining of all the definitions obtained inductively,
which may contain an
x\in c
as a parameter. So we have two families of potential definitions for
\Lambda
indexed by
x\in c
that we join together.
Now let us show that we have the desired semantic properties. First we need to show that

E
contains a definition for
\Lambda
under the right hypotheses, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},\exists x\in c.\;\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}},\Lambda\in\left(\left\{\left\{x\in c\mid\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\right\}\right\}~\cup~\bigcup\left\{E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\cup E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\mid x\in c\right\}\right)

(8)

which can be rephrased as

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},\exists x\in c.\;\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}},\Lambda=\left\{x\in c\mid\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\right\},\exists x\in c.\;\Lambda\in E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\cup E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}

Now concentrate on the statement

\Lambda=\left\{x\in c\mid\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\right\}
.
It would follow from
the two inclusions

\Lambda\subseteq\left\{x\in c\mid\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\right\}

and

\left\{x\in c\mid\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\right\}\subseteq\Lambda
, so, recalling that

\Lambda=\{x\in c\mid\lambda(x)\}
, the overall conclusion would follow from
having

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},x\in c\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},\exists x\in c.\;\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}},\lambda(x)\rightarrow\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}},\exists x\in c.\;\Lambda\in E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\cup E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}

and

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},x\in c\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},\exists x\in c.\;\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}},\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}\rightarrow\lambda(x),\exists x\in c.\;\Lambda\in E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\cup E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}

Those in turn follow from the following two statements

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},x\in c\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}},\neg\lambda(x),\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}},\Lambda\in E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\cup E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}

and

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},x\in c\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}},\neg\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}},\lambda(x),\Lambda\in E_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\cup E_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}

which are straightforward consequences of
5
and
4
respectively.
This concludes the proof of
8
.

Now we only need to prove a final property, which is

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\forall x\in c.\;\neg\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\vee\neg\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}

which is equivalent to the validity of

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}},x\in c\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\neg\theta_{1}^{\mathsf{IH}}\vee\neg\theta_{2}^{\mathsf{IH}}

which can be obtained by combining
6
and
7
with excluded
middle for
\rho(x,w)
.

–

If the principal formula is of the shape
\mathcal{G}=\exists r^{\prime}\in r~\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
where

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
begins with another existential quantifier and
\mathcal{G}
is a member of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}=\mathcal{G},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
,
the proof necessarily has shape

\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

then we can apply the induction hypothesis where in lieu of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
we have
\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
and
obtain
\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E^{\mathsf{IH}}
. It is then clear that we can simply set
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}
.

–

If the principal formula is not a member of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
, then we have two subcases corresponding to
whether the principal formula under consideration occurs in
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}
,
and whether the relevant membership statement that witnesses the instantiation
is a member of
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{R}}
. Let us list all of the different alternatives

*

If the last step of the proof has shape

\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}w\in b},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\varphi[w/x],\exists x\in b~\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}w\in b},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\exists x\in b~\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

with
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}={\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}^{\prime},w\in b}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}={\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime},\exists x\in b~\varphi}
,
we can conclude by setting
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}
, essentially because the set of free variables
L,R
and
C
can be taken
to be the same in the premise.

*

In the dual case where the last step has shape

\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}^{\prime},w\in b}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}^{\prime},\varphi[w/x],\exists x\in b~\varphi},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R},w\in b}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}^{\prime},\exists x\in b~\varphi},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}^{\prime},w\in b}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}={\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{R}^{\prime},\exists x\in b~\varphi}
,
we can also conclude immediately by setting
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}
.

*

If the last step has shape

\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}w\in b},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}^{\prime},\varphi[{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}w}/x],\exists x\in b~\varphi},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}w\in b},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}^{\prime},\exists x\in b~\varphi},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

with
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}={\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}^{\prime},w\in b}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}^{\prime},\exists x\in b~\varphi}
,
we need to do something non-trivial. We can still use the inductive hypothesis to obtain
\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E^{\mathsf{IH}}
, but they may feature variables

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
of
w
that are in
L
as free variables. But we also have that the free variables of
b
are included in
C
.
With that in mind, we can set
\theta\mathrel{:=}\exists x_{1}\ldots x_{n}|w\in b\,.\,\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E\mathrel{:=}\bigcup\left\{E^{\mathsf{IH}}\mid w\in b\right\}
.

*

If the last step has shape

\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R},w\in b}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime},\varphi[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}w}/x],\exists x\in b~\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}^{\prime},w\in b}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime},\exists x\in b~\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}^{\prime},w\in b}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}={\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime},\exists x\in b~\varphi}
,
we proceed similarly by setting
\theta\mathrel{:=}\forall x_{1}\ldots x_{n}|w\in b\,.\,\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E\mathrel{:=}\bigcup\left\{E^{\mathsf{IH}}\mid w\in b\right\}
.

•

The case of the
=
rule can be handled exactly as the
\top
rule.

•

For the
\neq
rule, we distinguish several subcases:

–

If we have
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}={\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\alpha[y/x],\Delta_{L}^{\prime}}

or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\alpha[y/x],\Delta_{R}^{\prime}}
, so that the
last step has one of the two following shapes

\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\alpha[y/x],\alpha[z/x],\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\alpha[y/x],\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}\qquad\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\alpha[y/x],\alpha[z/x],\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\alpha[y/x],\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

we can apply the induction hypothesis to obtain some
\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E^{\mathsf{IH}}
such that setting
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}
solves this
subcase; we leave checking the additional properties to the reader.

–

Otherwise, if we have

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}={\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta_{L}^{\prime}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\alpha[y/x],\Delta^{\prime}_{R}}
and a last step of
shape

\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\alpha[y/x],\alpha[z/x],\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\alpha[y/x],\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

In that case, the inductive hypothesis gives
\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E^{\mathsf{IH}}
with
free variables in
C\cup\{z\}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},\theta^{\mathsf{IH}},\Lambda\in E^{\mathsf{IH}}\quad\text{and}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\alpha[y/x],\alpha[z/x],\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\neg\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}

We then have two subcases according to whether
z\in C
or not

*

If
z\in C
, we can take
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge y=_{\mathfrak{U}}z
and
E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}
. Their free variables are in
C

and we only need to check

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge y=_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Lambda\in E^{\mathsf{IH}}\quad\text{and}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\alpha[y/x],\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\neg\theta^{\mathsf{IH}},y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z

which follow easily from the induction hypothesis.

*

Otherwise, we take
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}[y/z]
and
E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}[y/z]
.
In that case, note that we have
\alpha[z/x][y/z]=\alpha[y/x]
(which would not be necessarily the case if
z
belonged to
C
).
This allows to conclude that we have

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}[y/z],\Lambda\in E^{\mathsf{IH}}[y/z]\quad\text{and}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\alpha[y/x],\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\neg\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}[y/z]

directly from the induction hypothesis.

–

Otherwise, if we have

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}={\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\alpha[y/x],\Delta_{L}^{\prime}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta^{\prime}_{R}}
and a last step of
shape

\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\alpha[y/x],\alpha[z/x],\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\alpha[y/x],\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

In that case, the inductive hypothesis gives
\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E^{\mathsf{IH}}
with
free variables in
C\cup\{z\}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\alpha[y/x],\alpha[z/x],\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},\theta^{\mathsf{IH}},\Lambda\in E^{\mathsf{IH}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\neg\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}

We then have two subcases according to whether
z\in C
or not

*

If
z\in C
, we can take
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}\vee y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z
and
E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}
. Their free variables are in
C

and we only need to check

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\alpha[y/x],\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}\vee y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Lambda\in E^{\mathsf{IH}}\hskip 16.00008pt\text{and}\hskip 16.00008pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\neg\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}\wedge y=_{\mathfrak{U}}z

which follow easily from the induction hypothesis.

*

Otherwise, we take
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}[y/z]
and
E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}[y/z]
.
In that case, note that we have
\alpha[z/x][y/z]=\alpha[y/x]
(which would not be necessarily the case if
z
belonged to
C
).
This allows to conclude that we have

{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\alpha[y/x],\Delta_{L}^{\prime}},\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}[y/z],\Lambda\in E^{\mathsf{IH}}[y/z]\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}y\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}z,\Delta_{R}^{\prime}},\neg\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}[y/z]

directly from the induction hypothesis.

•

If the last rule applied is
\times_{\eta}
, the proof has shape

\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x]\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x],\tilde{\mathcal{G}}[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x]}{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}

and one applies the inductive hypothesis as expected to get
\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and

E^{\mathsf{IH}}
such that

\begin{array}[]{ll@{}l}&{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}}[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x]&\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}}[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x],\theta^{\mathsf{IH}},\Lambda[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x]\in E\\
\text{and}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}}[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x]&\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}}[\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle/x],\neg\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}\end{array}

and with free variables included in
C
if
x\notin C
or

\left(C\cup\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\right)\setminus\{x\}
otherwise.
In both cases, it is straightforward to check that taking

\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}[\pi_{1}(x)/x_{1},\pi_{2}(x)/x_{2}]
and

E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}[\pi_{1}(x)/x_{1},\pi_{2}(x)/x_{2}]
will yield the desired result.

•

Finally, if the last rule applied is the
\times_{\beta}
rule, it has shape

\dfrac{({\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}})[x_{i}/x]\vdash({\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime})[x_{i}/x]}{({\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Theta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Theta_{R}})[\pi_{i}(\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle)/x]\vdash({\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta_{L}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Delta_{R}},\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime})[\pi_{i}(\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle)/x]}

and we can apply the induction hypothesis to get satisfactory

\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}
and
E^{\mathsf{IH}}
(moving from
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
to
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}[x_{i}/x]
is unproblematic, as we can assume the
lemma works for
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
with arbitrary subformulas
\lambda
and
\rho
); it is easy to see that we can set
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta^{\mathsf{IH}}

and
E\mathrel{:=}E^{\mathsf{IH}}
and conclude.

This completes the proof of Lemma
22
.

5.3.
Proof of the main result

We now turn to the proof of our second main result, which we recall from the earlier
subsection:
See
16

We have as input a proof of

\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\wedge\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a}^{\prime},o^{\prime})\rightarrow o\equiv_{T}o^{\prime}

and we want an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression
E(\vec{i})
such that

\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}E(\vec{i})\equiv_{T}o

This will be a consequence of the following theorem.

Theorem 23
.

Given
\Delta_{0}

\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)
and
\psi(\vec{i},\vec{b},o^{\prime})

together with a
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized
proof with conclusion

\Theta(\vec{i},\vec{a},r);\;\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},r),\psi(\vec{i},\vec{b},o^{\prime})\vdash\exists{r^{\prime}\in_{p}o^{\prime}}.\;r\equiv_{T}r^{\prime}

we can compute in polynomial time an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression
E(\vec{i})
such that

\Theta(\vec{i},\vec{a},r);\;\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},r),\psi(\vec{i},\vec{b},o^{\prime})~\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}~r\in E(\vec{i})

That is, we can find an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
query that “collects answers”.
Assuming Theorem
23
, let’s prove the main result.

Proof 5.2

(Proof of Theorem
16
)
.

We assume
o
has a set type, deferring the simple product and Ur-element
cases (the latter using
get
) to Appendix
J
.
Fix an implicit definition of
o
up to extensionality relative
to
\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)
and a
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized proof of

\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\;\wedge\;\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a}^{\prime},o^{\prime})~\vdash~o\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T)}o^{\prime}

We can apply Lemma
19
, in the simple case where

p
is the “empty path”,
to obtain a
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized derivation of

\displaystyle r\in o;\;\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o),\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a}^{\prime},o^{\prime})\vdash~\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}\;r\equiv_{T}r^{\prime}

(9)

Then applying Theorem
23
gives
an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression
E(\vec{i})
such that

\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\wedge r\mathrel{\hat{\in}}o\wedge\;\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a}^{\prime},o^{\prime})~\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}~r\in E(\vec{i})

Thus, the object determined by
\vec{i}
is always contained in
E(\vec{i})
.
Recall that by (
9
), we have a derivation of

r\in o;\;\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\vdash\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a}^{\prime},o^{\prime})\rightarrow\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}\;r\equiv_{T}r^{\prime}

and applying interpolation (Theorem
18
) to that gives
a
\Delta_{0}
formula
\kappa(\vec{i},r)
such that the following are valid

\displaystyle r\in o\wedge\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\rightarrow\kappa(\vec{i},r)

(10)

\displaystyle\kappa(\vec{i},r)\wedge\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a}^{\prime},o^{\prime})\rightarrow\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{T}r^{\prime}

(11)

We claim that
E_{\kappa}(\vec{i})=\left\{x\in E(\vec{i})\mid\kappa(\vec{i},x)\right\}
is the
desired
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression. To show this, assume
\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)
holds.
We know already that
o\subseteq E(\vec{i})
and, by (
10
), every
r\in o
satisfies

\kappa(\vec{i},o)
, so
o\subseteq E_{\kappa}(\vec{i})
.
Conversely, if
x\in E_{\kappa}(\vec{i})
, we have
\kappa(\vec{i},x)
, so by (
11
),
we have that
x\in o
, so
E_{\kappa}(\vec{i})\subseteq o
. So
E_{\kappa}(\vec{i})=o
, which concludes the proof.

We now turn to the proof of Theorem
23
.

Proof of Theorem
23

We prove the theorem by induction over the type
T
.
We only prove the inductive step
for set types: the inductive case for products is straightforward.

For
T=\mathfrak{U}
, the base case of the induction, it is clear that we can take for
E
an expression computing the set of all
\mathfrak{U}
-elements
in the transitive closure of
\vec{i}
. This can clearly be done in
{\mathsf{NRC}}
.

So now, we assume
T={\sf Set}({T^{\prime}})
and that Theorem
23
holds up to
{T^{\prime}}
.
We have a
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized derivation of

\displaystyle\Theta;\;\varphi(\vec{i},r),\;\psi(\vec{i},o^{\prime})~\vdash~\exists{r^{\prime}\in_{p}o^{\prime}}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

(12)

omitting the additional variables for brevity.

From our input derivation, we can easily see that each element
of
r
must be equivalent to some element below
o^{\prime}
.
This is reflected in Lemma
19
, which allows us to
efficiently compute a proof of

\displaystyle\Theta,z\in r;\;\varphi(\vec{i},r),\;\psi(\vec{i},o^{\prime})~\vdash~\exists z^{\prime}\in_{mp}o^{\prime}.\;z\equiv_{T^{\prime}}z^{\prime}

(13)

We can then apply the inductive hypothesis of our main theorem at sort
{T^{\prime}}
,
which is strictly smaller than
{\sf Set}({T^{\prime}})
, on that new proof.
This yields an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression
E^{\mathsf{IH}}(\vec{i})

of type
{\sf Set}({T^{\prime}})
such that

\Theta,z\in r;\;\varphi(\vec{i},r),\psi(\vec{i},o^{\prime})\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}z\in E^{\mathsf{IH}}(\vec{i})

That is, our original hypotheses
entail
r\subseteq E^{\mathsf{IH}}(\vec{i})
.

Thus, we have used the inductive hypothesis to get a “superset expression”. But
now we want an expression that has
r
as an element. We will do this
by unioning a collection of definable subsets of
E^{\mathsf{IH}}(\vec{i})
.
To get these, we come back to our input derivation (
12
).
By Lemma
20
, we can efficiently compute a derivation of

\Theta;\;\varphi(\vec{i},r),\psi(\vec{i},o^{\prime})\vdash\exists r^{\prime}\in_{p}o^{\prime}\,\forall z\in a\;(z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r\leftrightarrow z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r^{\prime})

where we take
a
to be a fresh variable of sort
{\sf Set}({T^{\prime}})
.
Now, applying our
{\mathsf{NRC}}
Parameter Collection result (Theorem
21
)
we obtain an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression
E^{\mathrm{coll}}(\vec{i},a)
satisfying

\displaystyle\Theta;\;\varphi(\vec{i},r),\psi(\vec{i},o^{\prime})~\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}a\cap r\in E^{\mathrm{coll}}(\vec{i},a)

Now, recalling that we have
r\subseteq E^{\mathsf{IH}}(\vec{i})
and instantiating
a
to be

E^{\mathsf{IH}}(\vec{i})
, we can conclude that

\displaystyle\Theta;\;\varphi(\vec{i},r),\psi(\vec{i},o^{\prime})~\models_{{\mathsf{nested}}}r\in E^{\mathrm{coll}}(\vec{i},E^{\mathsf{IH}}(\vec{i}))

Thus we can take
E^{\mathrm{coll}}(\vec{i},E^{\mathsf{IH}}(\vec{i}))
as an explicit definition.

Complexity

Now let us sketch the complexity analysis of the underlying transformation.
The main induction is the type
T
of the object to be defined, and most of the
lemmas we use have a complexity that
depend on the size of the proofs, which is commensurate with the size of the proof
tree multiplied by the size of the input sequent, that we will write
n
. Let
us call
C(T,n)
the time complexity of our procedure
and show it can be taken to be polynomial. For the base case and the product case,
we have that
C(\mathfrak{U},n)
is
\mathcal{O}(n^{k})
for
k\geq 1
.
For set types
{\sf Set}(T)
, we first have a polynomial-time procedure in
n
to obtain
the new proof in Lemma
13
, and we have a recursive call
on this proof. Note that Lemma
19
also tells us
that this proof has size at most
n
, so the recursive call has complexity
at most
C(T,n)
. Then the subsequent transformations are simply polynomial-time
on the input proof, so we have for some exponent
k
large enough

C({\sf Set}(T),n)\leq C(T,n)+n^{k}

A similar analysis for products yields that

C(T_{1}\times T_{2},n)\leq C(T_{1},n)+C(T_{2},n)+n^{k}

All in all, if we call
s(T)
the size of a type defined in the obvious way,
we have

C(T,n)=\mathcal{O}(s(T)n^{k})

by induction on
T
, so since
s(T)\leq n
, the
overall time complexity is indeed polynomial in the size of the input derivation.
∎

6.
Discussion and future work

Our first contribution implies that whenever a set of
{\mathsf{NRC}}
views determines
an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
query, the query is rewritable over the views in
{\mathsf{NRC}}
.
By our second result, from a proof witnessing determinacy in our
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized proof system, we can efficiently generate the rewriting. Both results apply to a setting
where we have determinacy with respect to constraints and views, as in
Example
1
, or to general
\Delta_{0}
implicit definitions that
may not stem from views.

In terms of impact on databases, a crucial limitation of our work is that we do not yet
know how to find the proofs. In the case of relational data,
we know of many “islands of decidability” where proofs of determinacy can be found effectively – e.g.
for views and queries in guarded logics
[
BBtC18
]
.
But it remains open to find similar decidability results for views/queries in fragments of
{\mathsf{NRC}}
.

It is possible to use our proof system without full automation – simply search for a proof, and then
when one finds one, generate the rewriting. We have had some success with this approach in the relational setting,
where standard theorem proving technology can be applied
[
BKMT17
]
. But for the proof systems proposed
here, we do not have either our own theorem prover or a reduction to a system that has been implemented in the past.
The need to find proofs automatically is pressing since
our system is so low-level that it is difficult to do proofs by
hand. Indeed, a formal proof of implicit definability for Example
1
,
or even the simpler Example
5.1
,
would come to several pages.

In
[
BP21
]
, we introduce an intuitionistic version of our proof system,
and give a specialized algorithm for generating
{\mathsf{NRC}}
transformations from proofs of implicit
definability within this system. The algorithm for the intuitionistic case is considerably simpler
than for the proof systems we present here, and would probably make a good starting point for
an implementation of the system.

The implicit-to-explicit methodology requires
a proof of implicit definability, which implies implicit definability over all instances, not just finite ones.
This requirement is necessary:
one cannot hope to convert implicit definitions over finite instances
to explicit
{\mathsf{NRC}}
queries, even ineffectively. We do not believe that this
is a limitation in practice. See Appendix
A
for details.

For the effective Beth result, the key proof tool was the
{\mathsf{NRC}}
Parameter Collection theorem,
Theorem
21
. There is an intuition behind this
theorem that concerns a general setting,
where we have a first-order theory
\Sigma
that factors into a conjunction
of two formulas
\Sigma_{L}\wedge\Sigma_{R}
, and from this we have
