to get an interpolant
\theta_{1}
, and also
apply induction to our partitioned version
of
S_{2}
to get an interpolant
\theta_{2}
.
We
then put them together to get the interpolant for the partitioned sequent

S
. This “putting together”
will usually reflect the semantics of the connective mentioned in the proof rule.

Consider the case where the last rule applied is the
\neg
-L rule: this is the case
that motivates the more general invariant involving partitions.
We have a partition of the final sequent
\Theta;\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\Delta
.
We form a partition of the sequent
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash\neg\varphi,\Delta

by placing
\neg\varphi
on the same side (Left, Right) as
\varphi
was in the original
partition. We then get an interpolant
\theta
by induction. We just
use
\theta
for the final interpolant.

We consider the inductive case for
\wedge
-R. We have
two top sequents, one for each conjunct. We partition them in the obvious way:
each
\varphi_{i}
in the top is in the same partition that
\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
was
in the bottom. Inductively we take the interpolants

\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
for each sequent. We again do a case analysis based on
whether
\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
was in
\Delta_{L}
or in
\Delta_{R}
.

Suppose

\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
was in
\Delta_{R}
, so
\Delta_{R}=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2},\Delta^{\prime}_{R}
.
Then we arranged that each
\varphi_{i}
was
in
\Delta_{R}
in the corresponding top sequent. So we know
that
\Theta_{L};\Gamma_{L}\vdash\theta_{i},\Delta_{L}

and
\Theta_{R};\Gamma_{R},\theta_{i}\vdash\varphi_{i},\Delta^{\prime}_{R}
for
i=1,2
.
Now we can set the interpolant
\theta
to be

\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}
.

In the other case,
\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
was in
\Delta_{L}
, say
\Delta_{L}=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2},\Delta^{\prime}_{L}
.
Then we would arrange each
\varphi_{i}
to be “Left” in the corresponding top sequent, so we know that

\Theta_{L};\Gamma_{L}\vdash\theta_{i},\varphi_{i},\Delta^{\prime}_{L}

and
\Theta_{R};\Gamma_{R},\theta_{i}\vdash\Delta_{R}
for
i=1,2
.
We set
\theta=\theta_{1}\vee\theta_{2}
in this case.

With the
\exists
rule, a term in the inductively-assumed

\theta^{\prime}
for the top sequent may become illegal for the

\theta
for the bottom sequent, since it has a free variable that is not common. In this case, the term
in
\theta
is replaced by a quantified variable, where the
quantifier is existential or universal, depending on the
partitioning, and bounded according to the requirements
for
\Delta_{0}
formulas.
The non-common variables are then replaced and variable constraints are
added as described with the notation
\exists x_{1}\ldots x_{n}|t\in b\,.\,\varphi
and
\forall x_{1}\ldots x_{n}|t\in b\,.\,\varphi
on
p.
5.2.3
.

Appendix I
Proofs of polytime admissibility

The goal of this section is to prove most claims of polytime
admissibility made in the body of the paper, crucially those of
Subsection
5.3
.
Recall that a rule

\dfrac{\Theta\vdash\Delta}{\Theta^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\prime}}

is
polytime admissible
if we can compute in polynomial time
a proof of the conclusion
\Theta^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\prime}
from a proof of
the antecedent
\Theta\vdash\Delta
.

Throughout this section we deal with the
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized proof system of
Figure
3
.

I.1.
Standard rules

Here we collect some useful standard sequent calculi rules, which are all polytime
admissible in our system.
The arguments for these rules are straightforward.

Lemma 36
.

The following weakening rule is polytime admissible:

\dfrac{\Theta\vdash\Delta}{\Theta^{\prime}\vdash\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}}

Lemma 37
.

The following inference, witnessing the invertibility of the
\wedge
rule, is polytime admissible for both
i\in\{1,2\}
:

\dfrac{\Theta\vdash\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2},\Delta}{\Theta\vdash\varphi_{i},\Delta}

Lemma 38
.

The following, witnessing the invertibility of the
\forall
rule, is polytime admissible:

\dfrac{\Theta\vdash\forall x\in t.\varphi,\Delta}{\Theta,x\in t\vdash\varphi,\Delta}

Lemma 39
.

The following substitution rule is polytime admissible:

\dfrac{\Theta\vdash\Delta}{\Theta[t/x]\vdash\Delta[t/x]}

I.2.
Admissibility of generalized congruence

Recall the admissibility claim concerning the rule related to congruence:

Lemma 40
.

The following generalized congruence rule is polytime admissible:

\dfrac{\Theta[t/x,t/y]\vdash\Delta[t/x,t/y]}{\Theta[t/x,u/y]\vdash\neg(t\equiv u),\Delta[t/x,u/y]}

Recall that in a two-sided reading of this, the hypothesis
is

t\equiv u;\Theta[t/x,u/y]\vdash\Delta[t/x,u/y]
.
So the rule says that if we
\Theta
entails
\Delta
where both
contain
t
, then if we assume
t\equiv u
and substitute some occurrences of
t
with

u
in
\Theta
, we can conclude the corresponding substitution of
\Delta
.

To prove Lemma
40
in the case where the terms
t
and
u
are of type

{\sf Set}(T)
, we will need
a more general statement.
We are going to generalize the statement to treat tuples of terms and
use
{\mathsf{EL}}
formulas instead of
\neg(t\equiv u)
to simplify the inductive
invariant.

Given two terms
t
and
u
of type
t
, define by induction the set of formulas

\mathcal{E}_{t,u}
:

•

If
T=\mathfrak{U}
, then
\mathcal{E}_{t,u}
is
t\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}u

•

If
T=T_{1}\times T_{2}
, then

\mathcal{E}_{t,u}
is
\mathcal{E}_{\pi_{1}(t),\pi_{1}(u)},\mathcal{E}_{\pi_{2}(t),\pi_{2}(u)}

•

If
T={\sf Set}(T^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{E}_{t,u}
is
\neg(t\subseteq_{T}u),\neg(u\subseteq_{T}t)

The reader can check that
\mathcal{E}_{t,u}
is essentially
\neg(t\equiv u)
.

Lemma 41
.

The following rule is polytime admissible:

\dfrac{\Theta\vdash\Delta,\mathcal{E}_{t,u}}{\Theta\vdash\Delta,\neg(t\equiv u)}

Proof I.1
.

Straightforward induction over
T
.

Since we will deal with multiple equivalences, we will adopt vector notation

\vec{t}=t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
and
\vec{x}=x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
for lists of terms and
variables. Call
\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}}
the union of the
\mathcal{E}_{t_{i},u_{i}}
.
We can now state our more general lemma:

Lemma 42
.

The following generalized
n
-ary congruence rule for set variables is polytime admissible:

\dfrac{\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\vdash\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]}{\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}]\vdash\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}}}

Proof I.2

(Proof of Lemma
42
)
.

We proceed by induction over the proof of
\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\vdash\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}]
,

•

If the last rule applied is the
=
rule, i.e. we have

\dfrac{}{\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\vdash a[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]=_{\mathfrak{U}}b[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]}

and
a[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]=b[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]
, with
a,b
variables.
Now if
a
and
b
are equal, or if they belong both to either
\vec{x}
or
\vec{y}
,
it is easy to derive the desired conclusion with a single application of the
=
rule.
Otherwise, assume
a=x_{i}
and
b=y_{j}
(the symmetric case is handled similarly).
In such a case, we have that
\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}}
contains
t_{i}\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}t_{j}
. So the desired
proof

\dfrac{}{\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\vdash t_{i}=_{\mathfrak{U}}u_{j},\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}},t_{i}\neq_{\mathfrak{U}}t_{j}}

follows from the polytime admissibility of the axiom rule.

•

Suppose the last rule applied is the
\top
rule:

\dfrac{}{\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\vdash\top,\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]}

Then we do not need to apply the induction hypothesis. Instead we can immediately
apply the
\top
rule to obtain

\dfrac{}{\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}]\vdash\top,\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}}}

•

If the last rule applied is the
\wedge
rule

\dfrac{\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\vdash\varphi_{1}[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\hskip 18.49988pt\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\vdash\varphi_{2}[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]}{\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\vdash(\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2})[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]}

then the induction hypothesis gives us proofs of

\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}]\vdash\varphi_{i}[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}}

for both
i\in\{1,2\}
. So we can apply the
\wedge
rule to conclude that we have

\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}]\vdash(\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2})[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}}

as desired.

•

The cases of the rules
\vee
,
\forall
and
\times_{\eta}
are equally straightforward and left to the reader.

•

Now, let us handle the case of the
\exists
rule.

\dfrac{\Theta,t\in u\vdash\varphi[t/x],\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}}}{\Theta,t\in u\vdash\exists x\in u.\;\varphi,\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}}}

So assume that
z
is fresh wrt
\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{t},\vec{u},a,b
and
that the last step of the proof is

\dfrac{\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}],a[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\in b[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\vdash\varphi[a/z][\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}],\exists z\in c[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}].\;\varphi[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]}{\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}],a[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\in b[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]\vdash\exists z\in c[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}].\;\varphi[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]}

with
b[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]=c[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{t}/\vec{y}]
.
Set
a^{\prime}=a[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}]
,
b^{\prime}=b[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}]
,
c^{\prime}=c[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}]
.
We have three subcases:

–

If we have that
b^{\prime}=c^{\prime}
,
using the induction hypothesis, we have a proof of

\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],a^{\prime}\in b^{\prime}\vdash\varphi[a/z][\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\exists z\in c^{\prime}.\;\varphi[\vec{u}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}}

we can simply apply an
\exists
rule to that proof and we are done.

–

Otherwise, if we have that
b^{\prime}=t_{i}
and
c^{\prime}=u_{j}
for some
i,j\leq n
.
In that case,
extending the tuples
\vec{t}
and
\vec{u}
with
a^{\prime}
and a fresh variable

z^{\prime}
(the substitutions under consideration would be,
we can apply the induction hypothesis to obtain a proof of

\displaystyle\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y},a^{\prime}/z],a^{\prime}\in t_{i},z^{\prime}\in u_{j}\vdash

\displaystyle\varphi[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y},z^{\prime}/z],\exists z\in u_{j}.\;\varphi[\vec{u}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}},\mathcal{E}_{a^{\prime},z^{\prime}}

Note that
\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}}

contains an occurrence of
\neg(t_{i}\subseteq u_{j})
, which expands to

\exists z\in t_{i}.\forall z^{\prime}\in u_{j}.\neg(z\equiv z^{\prime})
, so we can construct the
partial derivation

\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y},a^{\prime}/z],a^{\prime}\in t_{i},z^{\prime}\in u_{j}\vdash\varphi[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y},z^{\prime}/z^{\prime}],\exists z\in u_{j}.\;\varphi[\vec{u}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}},\mathcal{E}_{a^{\prime},z^{\prime}}

\exists

\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],a^{\prime}\in t_{i},z^{\prime}\in u_{j}\vdash\exists z\in u_{j}.\varphi[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}},\mathcal{E}_{a^{\prime},z^{\prime}}

Lemma
41

\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y},a^{\prime}/z],a^{\prime}\in t_{i},z^{\prime}\in u_{j}\vdash\exists z\in u_{j}.\;\varphi[\vec{u}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}},a^{\prime}\equiv z^{\prime}

\forall

\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y},a^{\prime}/z],a^{\prime}\in t_{i}\vdash\exists z\in c^{\prime}.\;\varphi[\vec{u}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}},\forall z^{\prime}\in u_{j}.a^{\prime}\equiv z^{\prime}

\exists

\Theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y},a^{\prime}/z],a^{\prime}\in t_{i}\vdash\exists z\in c^{\prime}.\;\varphi[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\Delta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}],\mathcal{E}_{\vec{t},\vec{u}}

whose conclusion matches what we want.

–

Otherwise, we are in a similar case where
b^{\prime}=u_{j}
and
c^{\prime}=t_{i}
.
We proceed similarly, except that we use the formula
\neg(u_{j}\subseteq t_{i})

of
\mathcal{E}_{t_{i},u_{j}}
instead of
\neg(t_{i}\subseteq u_{j})
.

–

For the rule
\times_{\beta}
, which has general shape

\dfrac{\Theta[z_{i}/z]\vdash\Delta[z_{i}/z]}{\Theta[\pi_{i}(\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle)/z]\vdash\Delta[\pi_{i}(\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle)/z]}

we can assume, without loss of generality, that
z
occurs only once in
\Theta,\Delta
.
Let us only sketch the case where
z
occurs in a formula
\varphi
and the rule has shape

\dfrac{\Theta\vdash\varphi[z_{i}/z],\Delta}{\Theta\vdash\varphi[\pi_{i}(\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle)/z],\Delta}

We have that
\varphi[\pi_{i}(\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle)/z)]
is also of the shape
\psi[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}]
in our situation.
We can also assume without loss of generality that each variable in
\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
occur each a single time in
\Theta,\Delta
.
Now if we have a couple of situations:

*

If the occurrence of
z
do not interfere with the substitution
[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y}]
, i.e., there is a formula
\theta
such that

\varphi[\pi_{i}(\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle)/z]=\psi[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}]=\theta[\vec{t}/\vec{x},\vec{u}/\vec{y},\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle/z]

we can simply apply the induction hypothesis on the subproof and conclude with one application of
\times_{\eta}
.

*

If we have that
z
clashes with a variable of
\vec{x},\vec{y}
, say
x_{j}
, but that
t_{j}=v[\pi_{i}(\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle/x_{j})]
for some term
v
.
Then we can apply the induction hypothesis with the matching tuples of terms
\vec{t},x_{i}
and
\vec{u},t_{i}
and conclude by applying the
\beta
rule.

*

Otherwise, the occurrence of
z
does interfere with the substitution in such a way that we have,
say
t_{j}=\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle
. Then we can apply the induction hypothesis on the subproof with the matching tuples of terms
\vec{x},z_{i}
and
\vec{y},\pi_{i}(u_{j})

and conclude by applying the
\beta
rule.

One easy consequence of the above is Lemma
40
:

Proof I.3

(Proof of Lemma
40
)
.

Combine Lemma
42
and Lemma
41
.

Another consequence is the following corollary, which will be used later in this section:

Corollary 43
.

The following rule is polytime admissible:

\dfrac{\Theta,t\in u\vdash\Delta}{\Theta\vdash\neg t\mathrel{\hat{\in}}u,\Delta}

Proof I.4
.

Recall that
t\mathrel{\hat{\in}}u
expands to
\exists x\in u.\;x\equiv t
,
so that
\neg t\mathrel{\hat{\in}}u
is
\forall x\in u.\;\neg(x\equiv t)
.
So we have

\Theta,t\in u\vdash\Delta

Lemma
40

\Theta,x\in u\vdash\neg(x\equiv t),\Delta

\forall

\Theta\vdash\neg t\mathrel{\hat{\in}}u,\Delta

I.3.
Proof of Lemma
19

We now recall the claim of admissibility concerning rules
for “moving down in an equivalence”. These will make use of
the notation for quantifying on a path below an object, defined
in the body in Definition
5.1
.

See
19

Proof I.5
.

First, let us consider the simpler case where
p=\ni
.
We proceed by induction over the input proof of

\Theta\vdash\Delta,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

and make a case distinction
according to which rule was applied last. All cases are straightforward, save for one: when a
\exists
rule is applied on the formula
\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}
.
Let us only detail that one.

In that case, the last step has shape

\dfrac{\Theta,w\in o^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}w,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}}{\Theta,w\in o^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}}

Now observe that
r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}w
is not
{\mathsf{EL}}
, since if you unfold the macros it begins
with a universal. Thus the final rule that is applied in the proof witnessing the hypothesis sequent
cannot be the
\exists
rule.
We can thus infer that the expanded
proof tree ends with:

\Theta,w\in o^{\prime},z\in r\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}w,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

\Theta,w\in o^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},r\subseteq w,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

\vdots

\Theta,w\in o^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},w\subseteq r,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

\Theta,w\in o^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}w,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

\Theta,w\in o^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

In particular, we have a strictly smaller subproof of

\Theta,w\in o^{\prime},z\in r\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}w,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

Recall that
\mathrel{\hat{\in}}
is a macro: see Definition
3.1
.
Applying the induction hypothesis, we get a proof of

\Theta,w\in o^{\prime},z\in r\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}w,\exists z^{\prime}\in_{\ni,\ni}o^{\prime}.\;z\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}z^{\prime}

Applying the
\exists
-rule
we can then obtain a proof with conclusion

\Theta,w\in o^{\prime},z\in r\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},\exists r\in o^{\prime}.\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r,\exists z^{\prime}\in_{\ni,\ni}o^{\prime}.\;z\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}z^{\prime}

which concludes our argument, since
\exists r\in_{p}o^{\prime}.\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r
and

\exists z^{\prime}\in_{\ni,\ni}o^{\prime}.\;z\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}z^{\prime}
are syntactically the
same.

For the more complex case where
p
is non-trivial, we can conduct a similar
argument, at the cost of making the induction hypothesis more elaborate. We would
prove that the following rule is polytime admissible

\dfrac{\Theta,\Theta_{1},\ldots,\Theta_{n}\vdash\Delta,\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}}{\Theta,\Theta_{1},\ldots,\Theta_{n}\vdash\Delta,\exists z^{\prime}\in_{\ni,p}o^{\prime}.\;z\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}z^{\prime}}

Here
n
is any natural number and for each
i\leq n
, we have a decomposition of
p
as a concatenation
p_{i},p^{\prime}_{i}
, where
p^{\prime}_{i}
is non-empty and multiple
p_{i}
can be the same.
From this decomposition we define
\varphi_{i}=\exists z^{\prime}\in_{p^{\prime}_{i}}o_{i}.\;z\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}z^{\prime}
(with
o_{i}=o^{\prime}
iff
p_{i}=\varepsilon
) and

\Theta_{i}
is a context witnessing that
o_{i}\in_{p_{i}}o^{\prime}
. Formally,

\Theta_{i}=\Theta(p_{i},o_{i},o^{\prime})
with
\Theta(\varepsilon,x,y)=\cdot
,
\Theta(\ni,x,y)=x\in y
,

\Theta((p,\ni),x,y)=\Theta(p,x,z),z\in y
and
\Theta((p,j),x,y)=\Theta(p,x,\pi_{j}(y))
when
j\in\{1,2\}
.
The induction over a proof of the premise can be carried out in an analogous way to show admissibility, and the only non-trivial case, when a rule interacts
with one of the
\varphi_{i}
that has
p^{\prime}_{i}=\ni
, is handled in the same way as the non-trivial case for
p=\ni
.

I.4.
Proof of Lemma
20

See
20

Proof I.6
.

Much like what happened in the proof of
19
, the proof
can be done by induction over the shape of the input derivation when
p=\ni
.
When this is not the case, we can generalize our inductive hypothesis in a similar
way and use the same ideas. So for the sake of legibility, let us focus on
the case where
p=\ni
and go through the induction, making a case distinction according
to what was the last rule applied.

All cases are trivial, except for the case of the rule
\exists
, where

\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}
is the principal formula. So let us focus on that one.

In that case, the proof necessarily has shape

\Theta,y\in w\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},y\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

\forall

\Theta\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},w\subseteq_{T^{\prime}}r,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

\Theta,x\in r\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},x\mathrel{\hat{\in}}w,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

\forall

\Theta\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},r\subseteq_{T^{\prime}}w,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

\wedge

\Theta\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}w,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

\exists

\Theta\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\;r\equiv_{{\sf Set}(T^{\prime})}r^{\prime}

Applying the induction hypothesis to the leaves of that proof, we obtain two proofs
of

\displaystyle\Theta,y\in w\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},y\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\forall z\in a,\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r\leftrightarrow z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r^{\prime}

and

\displaystyle\Theta,x\in r\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},x\mathrel{\hat{\in}}w,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\forall z\in a,\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r\leftrightarrow z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r^{\prime}

so we can conclude using the admissibility of weakening and Corollary
43
twice and replaying the
\wedge/\forall/\exists
steps in the
appropriate order (a branch of the proof derivation is elided below for lack of horizontal space):

\Theta,y\in w\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},y\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\forall z\in a,\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r\leftrightarrow z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r^{\prime}

Corollary
43

\Theta\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},\neg(y\mathrel{\hat{\in}}w),y\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r,\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\forall z\in a,\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r\leftrightarrow z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r^{\prime}

\vee

\Theta\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},{y\mathrel{\hat{\in}}w}\rightarrow{y\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r},\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\forall z\in a,\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r\leftrightarrow z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r^{\prime}

\vdots

\Theta\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},{y\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r}\leftrightarrow{y\mathrel{\hat{\in}}w},\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\forall z\in a,\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r\leftrightarrow z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r^{\prime}

Lemma
36

\Theta,y\in a\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},{y\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r}\leftrightarrow{y\mathrel{\hat{\in}}w},\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\forall z\in a,\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r\leftrightarrow z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r^{\prime}

\forall

\Theta\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},\forall z\in a.\;{z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r}\leftrightarrow{z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}w},\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\forall z\in a,\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r\leftrightarrow z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r^{\prime}

\exists

\Theta\vdash\Delta^{\mathsf{EL}},\exists r^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}.\forall z\in a,\;z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r\leftrightarrow z\mathrel{\hat{\in}}r^{\prime}

Appendix J
Proof of the main theorem for non-set types

Recall again our main result:

See
16

In the body of the paper, we gave a proof for the case where the type of the defined object is
{\sf Set}(T)
for any
T
.
We now discuss the remaining cases: the base case and the inductive
case for product types.

So assume we are given an implicit
definition
\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)
and a
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized witness, and proceed by induction over the type
o
:

•

If
o
has type
{\mathsf{Unit}}
, then, since there is only one inhabitant in
type
{\mathsf{Unit}}
, then we can take our explicit definition to be the corresponding
{\mathsf{NRC}}
expression
\langle\rangle
.

•

If
o
has type
\mathfrak{U}
, then using interpolation on the entailment
\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\rightarrow(\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a}^{\prime},o^{\prime})\rightarrow o=_{\mathfrak{U}}o^{\prime})
,
we obtain
\theta(\vec{i},o)
with
\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\rightarrow\theta(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)
and
\theta(\vec{i},o)\wedge\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o^{\prime})\rightarrow o=_{\mathfrak{U}}o^{\prime}
.
But then we know that
\varphi
implies

o
is a subobject of
\vec{i}
: otherwise we could find a model that contradicts
the entailment.
There is an
{\mathsf{NRC}}
definition
A(\vec{i})
that collects all of the
\mathfrak{U}
-elements
lying beneath
\vec{i}
.
We can then take
E(\vec{i})=\textsc{get}_{T}(\{x\in A(\vec{i})\mid\theta(\vec{i},x)\})
as our

{\mathsf{NRC}}
definition of
o
. The correctness of
E
follows
from the properties of
\theta
above.

•

If
o
has type
T_{1}\times T_{2}
, recalling the definition of
\equiv_{T_{1}\times T_{2}}
, we have a derivation of

\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\wedge\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o^{\prime})\vdash\pi_{1}(o)\equiv_{T_{1}}\pi_{1}(o^{\prime})\wedge\pi_{2}(o)\equiv_{T_{2}}\pi_{2}(o^{\prime})

By Lemma
37
, we have proofs of

\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o)\wedge\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},o^{\prime})\vdash\pi_{i}(o)\equiv_{T_{i}}\pi_{i}(o^{\prime})

for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Take
o_{1}
and
o_{2}
to be fresh variables of types
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
. Take
\tilde{\varphi}(\vec{i},\vec{a},o_{1},o_{2})
to be
\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},\langle o_{1},o_{2}\rangle)
. By substitutivity (the admissible rule given
by Lemma
39
) and applying the
\times_{\beta}
rule, we have
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized proofs of

\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},\langle o_{1},o_{2}\rangle)\wedge\varphi(\vec{i},\vec{a},\langle o_{1}^{\prime},o_{2}^{\prime}\rangle)\vdash o_{i}\equiv_{T_{i}}o^{\prime}_{i}

We can apply our inductive hypothesis to obtain a definition
E^{\mathsf{IH}}_{i}(\vec{i})
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
.
We can then take our explicit definition to be
\langle E^{\mathsf{IH}}_{1}(\vec{i}),E^{\mathsf{IH}}_{2}(\vec{i})\rangle
.

Appendix K
Variant of Parameter Collection Theorem,

Theorem
21
, for parameterized definability
in first-order logic

Our paper has focused on the setting of nested relations, phrasing our results
in terms of the language
{\mathsf{NRC}}
. We indicated in the conclusion of the paper that
there is a variant of the
{\mathsf{NRC}}
parameter collection theorem, Theorem

21
, for the broader context of first-order logic.
In fact, this first-order version of the result provided the intuition
for the theorem.
In this section we present this variant.

We consider first-order logic with equality and without function symbols,
which also excludes nullary function symbols, that is, individual constants,
whose role is just taken by free individual variables. Specifically, we consider
first-order formulas with the following syntax

\varphi,\psi~~\mathrel{::=}~~P(\vec{x})\;|\;\neg P(\vec{x})\;|\;x=y\;|\;x\neq y\;|\;\top\;|\;\bot\;|\;\varphi\wedge\psi\;|\;\varphi\vee\psi\;|\;\forall x\,\varphi\;|\;\exists x\,\varphi.

Amongst the formulas given in the grammar above, those of the shape

P(\vec{x}),\neg P(\vec{x}),x=y
and
x\neq y
are called
literals
.
Literals come with a polarity: those of the shape
P(\vec{x})
and
x=y
are
positive while the other, of the shape
\neg P(\vec{x})
or
x\neq y
are
negative.

On top of this, we give some “syntactic sugar”. We define
\neg\varphi
by
induction over
\varphi
, dualizing every connective, including the
quantifiers, and removing doubled negation over literals. We define implication
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
as an abbreviation of
\neg\varphi\vee\psi
and bi-implication

\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
as an abbreviation of
(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\wedge(\psi\rightarrow\varphi)
. The set of free variables occurring in a formula
\varphi
is
denoted by
FV(\varphi)
and the set of predicates occurring in
\varphi
by

\mathit{PR}(\varphi)
.

Figure
5
shows our proof system for first-order logic.
It is identical to a system from the prior literature.
4
4

4

G3c+
Ref
+
Repl

[
NvP01
,
TS00
]
,
in the one-sided form of GS3, discussed in Chapter 3 of
[
TS00
]
, which
reduces the number of rules.
Like the
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized proof system we used in the body of the paper for
\Delta_{0}
formulas,
it is a
1
-sided calculus.
The formulas other than
\Gamma
in the premise are the
active
formulas
of the rule, while the
principal formulas
are the other
formulas in its conclusion. The complementary principal formulas in

Ax
have to be literals. The replacement of symbols induced by
equality with
Repl
is only performed on negative literals.

\small\par\frac{}{\vdash\Gamma,\varphi,\neg\varphi}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={Ax}, right={\hskip 9.00002pt$\varphi$ {a positive literal}}}\frac{}{\vdash\Gamma,\top}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={$\top$}}\frac{\vdash\Gamma,\varphi_{1}\vdash\Gamma,\varphi_{2}}{\vdash\Gamma,\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={$\wedge$}}\frac{\vdash\Gamma,\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}}{\vdash\Gamma,\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={$\vee$}}\\
\frac{\vdash\Gamma,\varphi[y/x]}{\vdash\Gamma,\forall x\;\varphi}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={$\forall$}, right={\hskip 9.00002pt$y\notin FV(\Gamma,\forall x\;\varphi)$}}\frac{\vdash\Gamma,\varphi[t/x],\exists x\;\varphi}{\vdash\Gamma,\exists x\;\varphi}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={$\exists$}}\\
\frac{\vdash t\neq t,\Gamma}{\vdash\Gamma}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={Ref}}\frac{\vdash t\neq u,\varphi[u/x],\varphi[t/x],\Gamma}{\vdash t\neq u,\varphi[t/x],\Gamma}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={Repl}, right={\hskip 9.00002pt$\varphi$ {a negative literal}}}

Figure 5.
One-sided sequent calculus for first-order logic with equality.

As in the body of the paper, a proof tree or derivation is a tree whose nodes are
labelled with sequents, such that the labels of the children of a given node
and that of the node itself are the premises and conclusion, resp., of an
instance of a rule from Figure
5
. The
conclusion
of a
proof tree is the sequent that labels its root. The proof system is closed
under cut, weakening and contraction. Closure under contraction in particular
makes it suited as basis for “root-first” proof search. Read in this
“bottom-up” way, the
\exists
rule states that a disjunction with an
existentially quantified formula can be proven if the extension of the
disjunction by a copy of the formula where the formerly quantified
variable
x
is instantiated with an arbitrary variable
t
can be proven. The
existentially quantified formula is retained in the premise and may be used to
add further instances by applying
\exists
again in the course of the proof.

Soundness of the rules is straightforward. For example the
\exists
rule
could be read as stating that if we deduce a disjunction in which one disjunct
is a formula
\varphi
with
t
in it, then we can deduce the same disjunction
but with some occurrences of
t
replaced in that disjunct with an
existentially quantified variable. Completeness of the proof system can also
be proven by a standard Henkin-style construction: indeed, since this
is really ordinary first-order logic, there are proofs in the literature
for systems that are very similar to this one
[
TS00
,
NvP98
]
.

Like our higher-level system in the body of the paper
(Figure
2
) the first-order system in Figure
5

does not impose any special constraints on the shape of the proof. We adapt
the extra conditions on contexts of the
{\mathsf{EL}}
-normalized system for
\Delta_{0}

formulas (Figure
3
) to our first-order system. We
characterize a proof in the system of Figure
5
as

FO-normalised
if no application of
Ax
,
\top
,
\exists
,

Ref
,
Repl
contains in its conclusion a formula whose
top-level connective is
\lor
,
\land
or
\forall
.

The FO-normalised property may be either incorporated directly into a
“root-first” proof procedure by constraining rule applications or it may be
ensured by converting an arbitrary given proof tree to a FO-normalised proof
tree with the same ultimate consequence.
This conversion is achieved by a straightforward adaption of the method for
the
\Delta_{0}
calculus from Appendix
G
,
Figure
4
. Only steps (1) and (2) of the method are
relevant here, since step (3) is specifically for the pair terms of

\Delta_{0}
formulas. In step (2), instances of
\lor
,
\land
or
\forall

are permuted down over
\exists
,
Ref
and
Repl
according
to the generic permutation schemas of Lemma
33
. The
left sides of
\vdash
in these schemas can be ignored since they represent
the
\in
-contexts for
\Delta_{0}
formulas. Finally, derivations of sequents
containing
\top
are replaced by applications of rule
\top
and derivations
of sequents containing complementary literals
\varphi,\lnot\varphi
by
applications of rule
Ax
. The conversion, however, may increase
the proof size exponentially as discussed in
Section
G.4
.

We now discuss our generalization of the
{\mathsf{NRC}}
Parameter Collection Theorem from the body of the paper to this
first-order setting.
The concept of explicit definition can
be generalized to
definition up to parameters and disjunction
: A family
of formulas
\chi_{i}(\vec{z},\vec{y},\vec{r})
,
1\leq i\leq n
, provides an

explicit definition up to parameters and disjunction
of a formula

\lambda(\vec{z},\vec{l})
relative to a formula
\varphi
if

\varphi\models\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\exists\vec{y}\forall\vec{z}\,(\lambda(\vec{z},\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\chi_{i}(\vec{z},\vec{y},\vec{r})).

(
\star
)

The entailment (

⋆

‣
K
) is considered with restrictions on the
predicates and variables permitted to occur in the
\chi_{i}
. In the simplest
case,
\lambda(\vec{z},\vec{l})
is a positive literal
p(\vec{z})
with a predicate
that is permitted in
\varphi
but not in the
\chi_{i}
. The predicate
p
is then
said to be
explicitly definable up to parameters and disjunction
with
respect to
\varphi

[
CK92
]
.

The disjunction over
a finite family of formulas
\chi_{i}
can be consolidated into a single
quantified biconditional as long as the domain
has size at least
2
in every model of
\varphi
.
Notice that if
\varphi
has only finite models, then by the
compactness theorem of first-order logic, the size of models must be bounded.
In such cases every formula
\lambda
is definable
with sufficiently many parameters.

We can now state our analog of the Parameter Collection Theorem, Theorem
21
.

Theorem 44
.

Let
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\lambda(\vec{z},\vec{l})
, and
\rho(\vec{z},\vec{y},\vec{r})
be first-order formulas such that

\varphi\land\psi\models\exists\vec{y}\forall\vec{z}\,(\lambda(\vec{z},\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\rho(\vec{z},\vec{y},\vec{r})).

Then there exist first-order formulas
\chi_{i}(\vec{z},{\vec{v}}_{i},{\vec{c}}_{i})
,
1\leq i\leq n
, such that

(1)

\varphi\land\psi\models\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\exists{\vec{v}}_{i}\forall\vec{z}(\lambda(\vec{z},\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\chi_{i}(\vec{z},{\vec{v}}_{i},{\vec{c}}_{i}))
,

(2)

{\vec{c}}_{i}\subseteq(FV(\varphi)\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\psi)\cup\vec{r})
,

(3)

\mathit{PR}(\chi_{i})\subseteq(\mathit{PR}(\varphi)\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\psi)\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho))
.

Moreover, given a FO-normalised proof of the precondition with the system of
Figure
5
, a family of formulas
\chi_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, with
the claimed properties can be computed in polynomial time in the size of the
proof tree.

In the theorem statement,
the free variables of
\lambda
are

\vec{z}
and
\vec{l}
, and the free variables of
\rho
are
\vec{z}
,
\vec{y}
, and
\vec{r}
.
The precondition supposes an explicit
definition
\rho
of
\lambda
up to parameters with respect to a conjunction

\varphi\land\psi
. The conclusion then claims that one can effectively compute
another definition of
\lambda
with respect to
\varphi\land\psi
that is up
to parameters and disjunction and has a constrained signature: free variables
and predicates must occur in at least one of the “left side” formulas
\varphi

and
\lambda
and also in at least one of the “right side” formulas
\psi

and
\rho
. In other words, the theorem states that if

{\mathcal{S}IG}_{L}
and
{\mathcal{S}IG}_{R}
are “left” and “right” signatures such that
\varphi

and
\lambda
are over
{\mathcal{S}IG}_{L}
,
\psi
and
\rho
are over
{\mathcal{S}IG}_{R}
, and

\rho
provides an explicit definition of
\lambda
up to parameters with
respect to
\varphi\land\psi
, then one can effectively compute another
definition of
\lambda
with respect to
\varphi\land\psi
that is up to
parameters and disjunction and is just over the intersection of the signatures

{\mathcal{S}IG}_{L}
and
{\mathcal{S}IG}_{R}
.

We now prove Theorem
44
by induction on the depth of the
proof tree, generalizing the constructive proof method for Craig interpolation
often called Maehara’s method
[
Tak87
,
TS00
,
Smu68b
]
. To simplify
the presentation we assume that the tuples
\vec{z}
and
\vec{y}
in the theorem
statement each consist of a single variable
z
and
y
, respectively. The
generalization of our argument to tuples of variables is straightforward.

To specify conveniently the construction steps of the family of formulas

\chi_{i}
we introduce the following concept: A
pre-defining
equivalence up to parameters and disjunction
(briefly
{\mathsf{PDEPD}}
) for a
formula
\lambda(z,\vec{l})
is a formula
\delta
built up from formulas of
the form
\forall z\,(\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\chi(z,\vec{p}))
(where the
left side is always the same formula
\lambda(z,\vec{l})
but the right sides

\chi(z,\vec{p})
may differ) and a finite number of applications of
disjunction and existential quantification upon variables from the
vectors
\vec{p}
of the right sides. The empty disjunction
\bot
is allowed
as a special case of a
{\mathsf{PDEPD}}
. By rewriting with the equivalence
\exists v\,(\delta_{1}\lor\delta_{2})\equiv\exists v\,\delta_{1}\lor\exists v\,\delta_{2}
, any
{\mathsf{PDEPD}}
for
\lambda
can be efficiently transformed into the
form
\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\exists\vec{v}_{i}\forall z\,(\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\chi_{i}(z,\vec{v}_{i},\vec{r}_{i}))
for some natural number
n\geq 0
. That is,
although a
{\mathsf{PDEPD}}
has in general not the syntactic form of the disjunction
of quantified biconditionals in the theorem statement (thus “pre-”), it
corresponds to such a disjunction. The more generous syntax will be convenient
in the induction.
The sets of additional variables
\vec{l}
and
\vec{p}
in the biconditionals

\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\chi(z,\vec{p})
can overlap, but the overlap will be
top-level variables that never get quantified. Although we have defined the
notion of
{\mathsf{PDEPD}}
for a general
\lambda
, in the proof we just consider

{\mathsf{PDEPD}}
s for the formula
\lambda
from the theorem statement.

For
{\mathsf{PDEPD}}
s
\delta
we provide analogs to
FV
and
\mathit{PR}
that only yield
free variables and predicates of
\delta
that occur in a right side of its
biconditionals, which helps to express the restrictions by definability
properties that constrains the signature of exactly those right sides. Recall
that we refer of these right sides as subformulas
\chi(z,\vec{p})
. For

{\mathsf{PDEPD}}

\delta
, define
\mathit{PR}^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\delta)
as the set of the predicate symbols
that occur in a subformula
\chi(z,\vec{p})
of
\delta
and define

FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\delta)
as the set of all variables that occur in a subformula

\chi(z,\vec{p})
of
\delta
and are free in
\delta
. In other words,

FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\delta)
is the set of all variables
p
in the vectors
\vec{p}
of
the subformulas
\chi(z,\vec{p})
that have an occurrence in
\delta
which is
not in the scope of a quantifier
\exists p
. If, for example
\delta=\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\exists\vec{v}_{i}\forall z\,(\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\chi_{i}(z,\vec{v}_{i},\vec{r}_{i}))
, then
FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\delta)
is the set of all
variables in the vectors
r_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
.

To build up
{\mathsf{PDEPD}}
s we provide an operation that only affects the right
sides of the biconditionals, a restricted form of existential quantification.
It is for use in interpolant construction to convert a free variable in right
sides that became illegal into an existentially quantified parameter. For
variables
p,y
define
\delta[y/p]^{\mathsf{RHS}}
as
\delta
after substituting
all occurrences of
p
that are within a right side
\chi(z,\vec{p})
and are
free in
\delta
with
y
. Define
\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}p\,\delta
as shorthand for

\exists y\,\delta[y/p]^{\mathsf{RHS}}
, where
y
is a fresh variable. Clearly

\delta\models\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}p\,\delta
and
p
has no free occurrences in
\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}p\,\delta
that are in any of the right side formulas
\chi(z,\vec{p})
, i.e.,

p\notin FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}p\,\delta)
. Occurrences of
p
in the left sides

\lambda(z,\vec{l})
, if
p
is a member of
\vec{l}
, are untouched in
\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}p\,\delta
. If
p
is not in
\vec{l}
, then
\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}p\,\delta
reduces to
ordinary existential quantification
\exists y\,\delta[y/p]
.

We introduce the following symbolic shorthand for the parametric definition on
the right side in the theorem’s precondition.

\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\hspace{1em}}lcl}\mathcal{G}&\mathrel{:=}&\exists y\forall z\,(\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\rho(z,y,\vec{r})).\hfil\hskip 10.00002pt\\
\end{array}

Note that our proof rules are such that if we have a proof (FO-normalised or
not) that our original top-level “global” parametric definition is implied
by some formula, then every one-sided sequent in the proof must include that
parametric definition in it. This is because the rules that eliminate a
formula when read “bottom-up” cannot apply to that parametric definition,
whose outermost logic operator is the existential quantifier. Thus, in our
inductive argument, we can assume that
\mathcal{G}
is always present.

We write

\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle,

where
\vdash\Gamma_{L},\Gamma_{R},\mathcal{G}
is a sequent, partitioned into three components,
multisets
\Gamma_{L}
and
\Gamma_{R}
of formulas and the formula
\mathcal{G}
from the
theorem’s hypothesis,
\theta
is a formula and
\mathcal{D}
is a
{\mathsf{PDEPD}}
, to
express that the following properties hold:

(1)

\models\Gamma_{R}\lor\theta
.

(2)

\models\lnot\theta\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\mathcal{D}
.

(3)

\mathit{PR}(\theta)\subseteq(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{L})\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{R})\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho))
.

(4)

FV(\theta)\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r})
.

(5)

\mathcal{D}
is a
{\mathsf{PDEPD}}
for
\lambda(z,\vec{l})
.

(6)

\mathit{PR}^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\mathcal{D})\subseteq(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{L})\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{R})\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho))
.

(7)

FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\mathcal{D})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r})
.

For a given proof with conclusion
\vdash\lnot\varphi,\lnot\psi,\mathcal{G}
,
corresponding to the hypothesis
\varphi\land\psi\models\mathcal{G}
of the
theorem, we show the construction of a formula
\theta
and
{\mathsf{PDEPD}}

\mathcal{D}

such that

\vdash\lnot\varphi;\;\;\lnot\psi;\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle.

From properties
1
–
2
and

5
–
7
it is then straightforward to read off
that the formula
\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\exists\vec{v_{i}}\forall\vec{z}(\lambda(\vec{z},\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\chi_{i}(\vec{z},{\vec{v}}_{i},{\vec{r}}_{i}))
obtained
from
\mathcal{D}
by propagating existential quantifiers inwards is as claimed in
the theorem’s conclusion.

Formula
\theta
plays an auxiliary role in the induction. For the overall
conclusion of the proof it a side result that is like a Craig interpolant
of
\psi
and
\varphi\rightarrow D
, but slightly weaker syntactically constrained
by taking
\lambda
and
\rho
into account:
FV(\theta)\subseteq((FV(\varphi)\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\psi)\cup\vec{r}))
and
\mathit{PR}(\theta)\subseteq(\mathit{PR}(\varphi)\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\psi)\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho))
.

As basis of the induction, we have to show constructions of
\theta
and

\mathcal{D}
such that
\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle
holds for

Ax
and
\top
, considering each possibility in which
the principal formula(s) can be in
\Gamma_{L}
or
\Gamma_{R}
. For the induction step,
there are a number of subcases, according to which rule is last applied and
which of the partitions
\Gamma_{L}
,
\Gamma_{R}
or
\mathcal{G}
contain the principal
formula(s). We first discuss the most interesting case, the induction step
where
\mathcal{G}
is the principal formula. This case is similar to the most
interesting case in the
{\mathsf{NRC}}
Parameter Collection Theorem, covered in the body
of the paper.

Case where the principal formula is
\mathcal{G}
.

We now give more detail on the most complex case. If the principal formula
of a conclusion
\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}
is
\mathcal{G}
, then the rule that is
applied rule must be
\exists
. From the FO-normalised property of the proof
it follows that the derivation tree ending in
\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}
must
have the following shape, for some
u\notin FV(\Gamma_{L},\Gamma_{R},\mathcal{G})
and
w\neq u
. Note that
u
could be either a top-level variable from
\lambda
,
i.e., a member of
\vec{l}
, or one introduced during the proof.

\begin{array}[]{rccrc}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\lor$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\lnot\lambda(u,\vec{l}),\Gamma_{R},\rho(u,w,\vec{r}),\mathcal{G}&&\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\lor$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\lambda(u,\vec{l}),\Gamma_{R},\lnot\rho(u,w,\vec{r}),\mathcal{G}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr\cline{5-5}\cr\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\land$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\Gamma_{R},\lambda(u,\vec{l})\rightarrow\rho(u,w,\vec{r}),\mathcal{G}&&&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\lambda(u,\vec{l}),\Gamma_{R},\rho(u,w,\vec{r})\rightarrow\lambda(u,\vec{l}),\mathcal{G}\\
\cline{2-5}\cr\lx@intercol\hfil\begin{array}[]{rc}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\forall$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\Gamma_{R},\lambda(u,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\rho(u,w,\vec{r}),\mathcal{G}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\exists$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\Gamma_{R},\forall z\,(\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\rho(z,w,\vec{r})),\mathcal{G}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\Gamma_{R},\mathcal{G}\end{array}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

The important point is that the two “leaves” of the above tree are both sequents
where we can apply our induction hypothesis.
Taking into account the partitioning of the sequents at the bottom
conclusion and the top premises in this figure, we can express the
induction step in the form of a “macro” rule that specifies the
how we constructed the required
\theta
and
\mathcal{D}
for the conclusion, making use of
the
\theta_{1},\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\theta_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}
that we get by applying the induction hypothesis to
each of the two premises.

\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{1em}}cl}\vdash\Gamma_{L},\lnot\lambda(u,\vec{l});\,\Gamma_{R},\rho(u,w,\vec{r});\,\mathcal{G}:\langle\theta_{1},\mathcal{D}_{1}\rangle\hfil\hskip 10.00002pt&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\lambda(u,\vec{l});\,\Gamma_{R},\lnot\rho(u,w,\vec{r});\,\mathcal{G}:\langle\theta_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle\\
\cline{1-2}\cr\lx@intercol\hfil\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle,\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

where
u
is as above. The values of
\theta
and
\mathcal{D}
– the new formula and definition
that we are building – will depend on occurrences of
w
, and
we give their construction in cases below:

(1)

If
w\notin FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}
or
w\in FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}
, then

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\theta&\mathrel{:=}&\forall u\,(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2}).\\
\mathcal{D}&\mathrel{:=}&\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{2}\lor\forall z\,(\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\theta_{2}[z/u]).\end{array}

(2)

Else it holds that
w\in FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}
and
w\notin FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}
. Then

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\theta&\mathrel{:=}&\forall w\forall u\,(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2}).\\
\mathcal{D}&\mathrel{:=}&\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}w\,(\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{2}\lor\forall z\,(\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\theta_{2}[z/u])).\end{array}

We now verify that
\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\rangle
, that
is, properties
1
–
7
, hold. The proofs for
the individual properties are presented in tabular form, with explanations
annotated in the side column, where
IH
stands for
induction
hypothesis
. We concentrate on the case
1
and indicate
the modifications of the proofs for case
2
in remarks,
where we refer to the values of
\theta
and
\mathcal{D}
for that case in terms
of the values for the case
1
as
\forall w\,\theta
and

\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}w\,\mathcal{D}
. In the proofs of the semantic
properties
1
and
2
we let sequents
stand for the disjunction of their members.

Property
1
:

\begin{array}[]{Z{1.8em}@{\hspace{1em}}l@{\hspace{0.5em}}l}\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{r-theta-1}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\Gamma_{R}\lor\rho(u,w,\vec{r})\lor\theta_{1}.\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{r-theta-2}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\Gamma_{R}\lor\lnot\rho(u,w,\vec{r})\lor\theta_{2}.\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{r-theta-3}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\Gamma_{R}\lor\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2}.\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by~(??)\immediate, (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{r-theta-4}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\Gamma_{R}\lor\forall u\,(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{1})\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by~(??)\immediate since }u\notin FV(\Gamma_{R})\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{r-theta-5}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\Gamma_{R}\lor\theta.\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by~(??)\immediate and def. of }\theta\\
\end{array}

For case
2
, it follows from the precondition
w\notin FV(\Gamma_{R})
and step (??) that
\models\Gamma_{R}\lor\forall w\,\theta
.

Property
2
:

\begin{array}[]{Z{1.8em}@{\hspace{1em}}l@{\hspace{0.5em}}l}\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{l-theta-i1}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\lnot\theta_{1}\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\lnot\lambda(u,\vec{l})\lor\mathcal{D}_{1}.\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{l-theta-i2}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\lnot\theta_{2}\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\lambda(u,\vec{l})\lor\mathcal{D}_{2}.\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{l-theta-3}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\lnot(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2})\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\lnot\lambda(u,\vec{l})\lor\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\theta_{2}.\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{l-theta-4}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\lnot(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2})\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\lnot\lambda(u,\vec{l})\lor\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\mathcal{D}_{2}\lor\theta_{2}.\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{l-theta-5}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\lnot\forall u\,(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2})\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\lnot\lambda(u,\vec{l})\lor\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\mathcal{D}_{2}\lor\theta_{2}.\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{l-theta-6}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\lnot\forall u\,(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2})\lor\lnot\theta_{2}\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\lambda(u,\vec{l})\lor\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\mathcal{D}_{2}.\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{l-theta-7}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\lnot\forall u\,(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2})\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\mathcal{D}_{2}\;\lor\hfil\hskip 5.0pt\\
\hskip 0.0pt$$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\hskip 20.00003pt(\lambda(u,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\theta_{2}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate, (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{l-theta-8}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\lnot\forall u\,(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2})\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{2}\;\lor\hfil\hskip 5.0pt\\
\hskip 0.0pt$$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\hskip 20.00003pt(\lambda(u,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\theta_{2}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{l-theta-9}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\lnot\forall u\,(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2})\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{2}\;\lor\hfil\hskip 5.0pt\\
\hskip 0.0pt$$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\hskip 20.00003pt\forall z\,(\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\theta_{2}[z/u]).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate}\textrm{ since }u\notin FV(\Gamma_{L},\lambda(z,\vec{l}))\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{l-theta-10}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\models\lnot\theta\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\mathcal{D}.\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate, defs. }\theta,\mathcal{D}\\
\end{array}

That
u\notin FV(\lambda(z,\vec{l}))
follows from the precondition
u\notin FV(\mathcal{G})
. It is used in step (??) to justify that the substitution

[z/u]
has only to be applied to
\theta_{2}
and not to
\lambda(z,\vec{l})
and, in
addition, to justify that
u\notin FV(\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{1})
and
u\notin FV(\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{2})
, which follow from
u\notin FV(\lambda(z,\vec{l}))
and the
induction hypotheses that property
5
applies to
\mathcal{D}_{1}

and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
.

For case
2
, it follows from step (??) that

\models\lnot\forall w\,\theta\lor\Gamma_{L}\lor\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}w\,\mathcal{D}
.

Property
3
:

\begin{array}[]{Z{1.8em}@{\hspace{1em}}l@{\hspace{0.5em}}l}\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{sig-theta-1}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\mathit{PR}(\theta_{1})\subseteq(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{L},\lnot\lambda(u,\vec{l}))\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{R},\rho(u,w,\vec{r}))\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho)).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{sig-theta-2}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\mathit{PR}(\theta_{2})\subseteq(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{L},\lambda(u,\vec{l}))\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{R},\lnot\rho(u,w,\vec{r}))\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho)).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{sig-theta-3}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\mathit{PR}(\forall u\,(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{1}))\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\hskip-5.0pt\textrm{by (??)\immediate, (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{sig-theta-4}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\mathit{PR}(\theta)\subseteq(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{L})\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{R})\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho))\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\hskip-5.0pt\textrm{by (??)\immediate, def. }\!\theta\end{array}

For case
2
the property follows since
\mathit{PR}(\theta)=\mathit{PR}(\forall w\,\theta)
.

Property
4
:

\begin{array}[]{Z{1.8em}@{\hspace{1em}}l@{\hspace{0.5em}}l}\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-theta-1}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV(\theta_{1})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L},\lnot\lambda(u,\vec{l}))\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R},\rho(u,w,\vec{r}))\cup\vec{r}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-theta-2}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV(\theta_{2})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L},\lambda(u,\vec{l}))\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R},\lnot\rho(u,w,\vec{r}))\cup\vec{r}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-theta-x1}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV(\theta_{1})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}\cup\{u\})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}\cup\{u,w\}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-theta-x2}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV(\theta_{1})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}\cup\{u\})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}\cup\{u,w\}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-theta-3}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV(\forall u\,(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2}))\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate, (??)\immediate},\\
\hskip 0.0pt$$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\lx@intercol\hfil\textrm{and
$w\notin FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}$ or $w\in FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}$}\lx@intercol\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-theta-4}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV(\theta)\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate, def. }\theta\\
\end{array}

For case
2
step (??) has to be replaced by

FV(\forall w\forall u\,(\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2}))\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}),

which follows just from (??) and
(??). Instead of step (??) we then have

FV(\forall w\,\theta)\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r})
.

Property
5
: Immediate from the induction hypothesis and the
definition of
\mathcal{D}
.

Property
6
:

\begin{array}[]{Z{1.8em}@{\hspace{1em}}l@{\hspace{0.5em}}l}\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{sig-d-1}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\mathit{PR}^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\mathcal{D}_{1})\subseteq(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{L},\lnot\lambda(u,\vec{l}))\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{R},\rho(u,w,\vec{r}))\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho)).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{sig-d-2}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\mathit{PR}^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\mathcal{D}_{2})\subseteq(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{L},\lambda(u,\vec{l}))\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{R},\lnot\rho(u,w,\vec{r}))\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho)).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{sig-d-3}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\mathit{PR}(\theta_{2})\subseteq(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{L},\lambda(u,\vec{l}))\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{R},\lnot\rho(u,w,\vec{r}))\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho)).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{sig-d-4}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\mathit{PR}^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{2}\lor\forall z\,(\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\theta_{2}[z/u]))\;\subseteq\hfil\hskip 5.0pt\\
\hskip 0.0pt$$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{L})\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{R})\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho))\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\hskip-30.00005pt\textrm{by (??)\immediate--(??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{sig-d-5}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\mathit{PR}^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\mathcal{D})\subseteq(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{L})\cup\mathit{PR}(\lambda))\cap(\mathit{PR}(\Gamma_{R})\cup\mathit{PR}(\rho))\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\hskip-30.00005pt\textrm{by (??)\immediate, def. }\mathcal{D}\end{array}

For case
2
the property follows since
\mathit{PR}^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\mathcal{D})=\mathit{PR}^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}w\,\mathcal{D})
.

Property
7
:

\begin{array}[]{Z{1.8em}@{\hspace{1em}}l@{\hspace{0.5em}}l}\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-d-1}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\mathcal{D}_{1})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L},\lnot\lambda(u,\vec{l}))\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R},\rho(u,w,\vec{r}))\cup\vec{r}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-d-2}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\mathcal{D}_{2})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L},\lambda(u,\vec{l}))\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R},\lnot\rho(u,w,\vec{r}))\cup\vec{r}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-d-3}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV(\theta_{2})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L},\lambda(u,\vec{l}))\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R},\lnot\rho(u,w,\vec{r}))\cup\vec{r}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{IH}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-d-1x}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\mathcal{D}_{1})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}\cup\{u\})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}\cup\{u,w\}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-d-2x}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\mathcal{D}_{2})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}\cup\{u\})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}\cup\{u,w\}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-d-3x}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV(\theta_{2})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}\cup\{u\})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}\cup\{u,w\}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate}\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-d-4}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{2}\lor\forall z\,(\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\theta_{2}[z/u]))\;\subseteq\hfil\hskip 5.0pt\\
\hskip 0.0pt$$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate, (??)\immediate, (??)\immediate,}\\
\hskip 0.0pt$$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&\lx@intercol\hfil\textrm{and $w\notin FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}$
or $w\in FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}$}\lx@intercol\\
\hskip 0.0pt${\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}{fv-d-5}$\@add@raggedleft\hskip 10.00002pt&FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\mathcal{D})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}).\hfil\hskip 5.0pt&\textrm{by (??)\immediate, def. }\mathcal{D}\end{array}

For case
2
step (??) has to be replaced by

\begin{array}[]{l}FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}w(\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}u\,\mathcal{D}_{2}\lor\forall z\,(\lambda(z,\vec{l})\leftrightarrow\theta_{2}[z/u])))\;\subseteq\\
(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}),\end{array}

which follows just from
(??), (??), (??). Instead of
step (??) we then have
FV^{\mathsf{RHS}}(\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}w\,\mathcal{D})\subseteq(FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l})\cap(FV(\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r})
.

This completes the verification of correctness, and thus ends our discussion of this
case.

We now turn to the base of the induction along with the other inductive cases.

Cases where the principal formulas are in the

\Gamma_{L}
or
\Gamma_{R}
partition.

The inductive cases where the principal formulas are in the
\Gamma_{L}
or
\Gamma_{R}

partition can be conveniently specified in the form of rules that lead from
induction hypotheses of the form
\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle

as premises to an induction conclusion of the same form. Base cases can
be taken as rules without premises. The axioms and rules shown below
correspond to those of the calculus, but replicated for each possible way
in which the partitions
\Gamma_{L}
,
\Gamma_{R}
or
\mathcal{G}
of the conclusion can contain the
principal formula(s). To verify that
properties
1
–
7
are preserved by each of the
constructions is straightforward, and therefore we only
point out a few subtleties.

(1)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small{Ax}}&&\hskip 20.00003pt\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\varphi\textit{ a positive literal}$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\varphi,\lnot\varphi;\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\top,\bot\rangle\\
\end{array}

(2)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small{Ax}}&&\hskip 20.00003pt\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\varphi\textit{ a positive literal}$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R},\varphi,\lnot\varphi;\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\bot,\bot\rangle\end{array}

(3)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small{Ax}}&&\hskip 20.00003pt\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\varphi\textit{ a literal}$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\varphi;\;\;\Gamma_{R},\lnot\varphi;\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\varphi,\bot\rangle\end{array}

(4)

\begin{array}[]{rc}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\top$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\top;\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\top,\bot\rangle\end{array}

(5)

\begin{array}[]{rc}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\top$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R},\top;\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\bot,\bot\rangle\end{array}

(6)

\begin{array}[t]{rc}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\lor$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\end{array}

(7)

\begin{array}[t]{rc}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\lor$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R},\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\end{array}

(8)

\begin{array}[t]{rc@{\hspace{2em}}c}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\land$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\varphi_{1};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta_{1},\mathcal{D}_{1}\rangle\hfil\hskip 20.00003pt&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\varphi_{2};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle\\
\cline{2-3}\cr&\lx@intercol\hfil\vdash\Gamma_{L},\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta_{1}\land\theta_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

(9)

\begin{array}[t]{rc@{\hspace{2em}}c}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\land$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R},\varphi_{1};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta_{1},\mathcal{D}_{1}\rangle\hfil\hskip 20.00003pt&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R},\varphi_{2};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle\\
\cline{2-3}\cr&\lx@intercol\hfil\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R},\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta_{1}\lor\theta_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1}\lor\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

(10)

\begin{array}[t]{rc}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\exists$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\varphi[t/x],\exists x\,\varphi;\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\exists x\,\varphi;\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\rangle,\end{array}

where the values of
\theta^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
depend on occurrences of
t
:

•

If
t\in FV(\Gamma_{L},\exists x\,\varphi)\cup\vec{l}
, then
\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\theta
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}
.

•

Else it holds that
t\notin FV(\Gamma_{L},\exists x\,\varphi)\cup\vec{l}
.
Then
\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\exists t\,\theta
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}t\,\mathcal{D}
.

(11)

\begin{array}[t]{rc}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\exists$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R},\varphi[t/x],\exists x\,\varphi;\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R},\exists x\,\varphi;\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\rangle,\end{array}

where the values of
\theta^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
depend on occurrences of
t
:

•

If
t\in FV(\Gamma_{R},\exists x\,\varphi)\cup\vec{r}
, then

\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\theta
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}
.

•

Else it holds that
t\notin FV(\Gamma_{R},\exists x\,\varphi)\cup\vec{r}
. Then

\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\forall t\,\theta
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\exists^{\mathsf{RHS}}t\,\mathcal{D}
.

(12)

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\forall$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\varphi[y/x];\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle&\hskip 20.00003pt\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$y\notin FV(\Gamma_{L},\forall x\,\varphi,\Gamma_{R},\mathcal{G})$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L},\forall x\,\varphi;\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\end{array}

(13)

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small$\forall$}&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R},\varphi[y/x];\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle&\hskip 20.00003pt\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$y\notin FV(\Gamma_{L},\Gamma_{R},\forall x\,\varphi,\mathcal{G})$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R},\forall x\,\varphi;\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\end{array}

(14)

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small{Ref}}&\vdash t\neq t,\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle&\hskip 20.00003pt\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$t\in FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\end{array}

(15)

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small{Ref}}&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;t\neq t,\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle&\hskip 20.00003pt\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$t\notin FV(\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\end{array}

(16)

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small{Repl}}&\vdash t\neq u,\varphi[u/x],\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle&\hskip 20.00003pt\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\varphi\textit{ a negative literal}$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash t\neq u,\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{L};\;\;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\end{array}

(17)

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small{Repl}}&\vdash t\neq u,\Gamma_{L};\;\;\varphi[u/x],\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle&\hskip 20.00003pt\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\varphi\textit{ a negative literal}$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash t\neq u,\Gamma_{L};\;\;\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\rangle,\end{array}

where the values of
\theta^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
depend on occurrences of
t
and
u
:

•

If
t\notin FV(\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}
, then
\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\theta
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}
. In this subcase the precondition
t\notin FV(\varphi[t/x])
implies that
x\notin FV(\varphi)
and thus

\varphi[u/x]=\varphi[t/x]
.

•

If
t,u\in FV(\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}
, then
\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\theta\lor t\neq u
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}
.

•

Else it holds that
t\in FV(\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}
and
u\notin FV(\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{R})\cup\vec{r}
. Then
\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\theta[t/u]

and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}[t/u]^{\mathsf{RHS}}
. For this subcase, to derive
property
1
it is used that the precondition
u\notin\varphi[t/x]
implies that
\varphi[u/x][t/u]=\varphi[t/x]
.

(18)

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small{Repl}}&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;t\neq u,\varphi[u/x],\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle&\hskip 20.00003pt\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\varphi\textit{ a negative literal}$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\Gamma_{L};\;\;t\neq u,\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle\end{array}

(19)

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\small{Repl}}&\vdash\varphi[u/x],\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{L};\;\;t\neq u;\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta,\mathcal{D}\rangle&\hskip 20.00003pt\raisebox{-8.61108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\varphi\textit{ a negative literal}$}\\
\cline{2-2}\cr&\vdash\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{L};\;\;t\neq u,\Gamma_{R};\;\;\mathcal{G}\;:\;\langle\theta^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\rangle,\end{array}

where the values of
\theta^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
depend on occurrences of
t
and
u
:

•

If
t\notin FV(\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}
, then
\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\theta
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}
. In this subcase the precondition
t\notin FV(\varphi[t/x])
implies that
x\notin FV(\varphi)
and thus

\varphi[u/x]=\varphi[t/x]
.

•

If
t,u\in FV(\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}
, then
\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\theta\land t=u
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}
.

•

Else it holds that
t\in FV(\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}
and
u\notin FV(\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{L})\cup\vec{l}
. Then
\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\theta[t/u]

and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}[t/u]^{\mathsf{RHS}}
. To derive property
2

for this subcase, that is,
\models\lnot\theta[t/u]\lor\varphi[t/x],\Gamma_{L}\lor\mathcal{D}[t/u]^{\mathsf{RHS}}
, it is required that
u\notin FV(\mathcal{D}[t/u]^{\mathsf{RHS}})
, which follows from the precondition
u\notin\vec{l}
of the subcase.

This completes the proof of Theorem
44
.
